A BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN.

The information contained in this book ought to be known by every married woman, and It will not harm the unmarried to read. The book is c nveniently divided into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty, or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out the best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful Information that one can ordinarily get only from an intelhgent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood r, tattoos; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth chapter treats of mishaps and how to avoid them The seventh chapter treats of maternal Impressions and shows that birthmarks are not due to longings tn the part of the mother, but rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter &aches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy 1 certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby Is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is up and ilhaut again. The eleventh chapter treats of sterility ; ices the main causes of it, bow these may be overcome and chlbiren result The last chapter treats of the • change," a most irnpodant article for al women over focty. The book is lud of useful information, and no book is written which goes so thoroughly into matter. relating to married women Some may think too much is told ; such can scarcely be the Ca e. for knowledge is power and the means ot attaioing happiness. The book can be had from Dr. T. R. ALLINSON, 25 Box, 4, Spanish-place, Manchester-square, London. W., in return for a Foetal Order for ls.

A Game OF STRENGTH. —Society has a new diversion known as parlour football. It is played on a large table, the football being an eggshell, from which the contents have been removed through a small hole in one end. The goals are opposite ends Of the table, and the egg is propelled by blowing, instead of I-icking. In a game of this kind the person who has previously eaten an onion invariably wins.

"CAPITAL" CHAT.

The Carlton Club will soon have to keep an extra I messenger to look after the cycles of members. The other day Pall Mall was amazed to see a string. of well-known Conservatives ride up to their club on bicycles to get their letters. The cabmen on the rank were clearly very angry. Mr. Balfour has done something to set the fashion,alt hough he Only rides in private; and among the bicyclists were Sir John G.,rst and Sir Herbert Maxwell. The latter left his machine outside the building,but Sir John took his through the sacred portals of the club. We have long known, however, that Sir John is a bit of a leveller.

In the light of recent developmects. and having particular regard to the relationship subsisting be- tween Russia and France, it is .of extreme interest to learn that the Czar's Government have deter- mined to make large purchases of Suez Canal shares. To this end a sum approximating twenty millions sterling is to be provided during the next t hree or four years; and already a firm of Russian agents in Paris, in close alliance with the Bank of France, have received instructions for the purchase of any large parcel of shares which may come upon the market.

Mr. Baiilia of Dochfour, who now becomes mem- ber for Inverness-shire, ranks abreast of Earl &a- field, Lord Macdonald, and Lord Lovat in the ex- tent of his possessions in that county. They each own about one hundred and sixty thousand acres. Next in order come The Mackintosh of Mackintosh, Sir George Macpherson Grant of Ballindalloc , Lochiel, and The Chisholm. In point of acreage only, however, is Mr. Baillie abreast of these High- land chiefs. The territorial greatness of the Baillies is a comparatively modern affair.

Lancashire members on both sides of the House of Commons are prepared to raise a protest against the proposal to charge to India the cost of enter- taming the Shahzada in this country. They oppose the proposal on the general ground that such a policy is unworthy of a great and wealthy country like this, and on the particular ground that, having regard to the Indian cotton duties, the cost must fall substantially in the end upon Lancashire.

Sir Wilfrid Lawson frequently tells a good story, and often he is himself the hero of it. At a re- cent Veto Bill demonstration in Essex, Sir Wilfrid, acknowledging a vote of thanks, remarked that somebody had said he was "venerable," and some- body else that he was " gallant." The one term had something clerical about it,and the other some- thing military, and neither the one nor the other was anything in his line. "I will tell you,' con- tinued Sir Wilfrid, "what I am. There was a school in the North of England, and the master gave the children a long disquisition upon the steam-engine and when he thought they all under- stood it, he asked, What is it that does the work of forty horses, and drinks nothing but water?' land they called out, Sir Wilfrid Lawson!'"

"An Old Liberal writing to the " London Daily Chronicle' says :—" The recent discussions in the louse of Commons over the proposal to erect a statute to Oliver Cromwell recall to my recollection a similar controversy which raged in the later for- Liss or the earlier fifties. The present Houses of Parliament were nearly completed, and ir was pro- posed to ornament A estminater Hall with statues of the Kings and Queens of England. A very few years before, the reputation of the Great Protector had been rehabilitated by the publication of Carlyle's " Cromwell'B Letters and Speeches." The question arose whether Cromwell should take up his position amongst the long line of monarchs who have de facto if not de jure reigned ever Great Britian. ' Irish feeling did not count for very much in those days, either in Parliament or society. The politics of the Sister Isle were suffering from the reaction which followed the physical force demonstrations of Smith O'Brien, Mitchell and Meagher.

"But opponents were not wanting to any sug- gestion that honour should be done to the memory of the Great Protector. The policy and the methods of Cromwell were as objectionable to Whigs of the Lord John Russell and Lord Palmerston school as to Tories like the Earl of Derby and Sir Robert Peel. There was many Liberals and Radicals, both in Par- liament and the Press, to do battle for 'the greatest of Englishmen,' but the proposal te erect a monu- ment to the uncrowned king fell to the ground. I can only regret that Liberalism to-day is as power- less as it was nearly half a century ago to ensure recognition for one who, whatever his faults and foibles, was one of the founders of our civil and re- ligious liberty."

When the Bulldog Club was formed in 1875 to rescue the national breed from the extinction which then threatened it its most sanguine promoters could hardly have anticipated the success which has been attained in two decades. The number of dogs available for a show at that date was only 30, as against 241 entries at the exhibition opened by the Club On Tuesday at the Westminster Aquurium. "The gentleman's lap-dog," as it has been termed by one of the best judges, has now be- come the favourite of ladies, the brisk demand for these animals being the main cause, doubtless, of the proximity to perfection of breeds which marks Ithe show new under notice. The quality of the bulldogs exhibited on Tuesday is quite exceptional, a large majority of the entries closely approaching the Club standard in their combination of strength, determination, and activity, not wholly un,ugges- tive of the Highland bull. The honours of cham- pionship were assigned by the judges to 'Mr. Jack Ellis's Guido, a marvellously grand-headed dog well known in Manchester, and to Cigarette, the winner of 120 first prizes, and. daughter of the famous Don Pedro, to whom was accorded the honour of a public funeral in Hyde Park in Feb- ruary last.

The calculations as to the effective Ministerialis majority have varied a good deal, hut I am afraid the truth is really more alarming than any of the current statements indicate. Here is the actual position of things when the figures are carefully worked out. The Government started with a majority of forty. They have lost five seats, a loss which reduces it to thirty, and the change in the Speakership has cost them two more votes, as this sacrificed an effective vote in the case of Mr. Gully, while the Leamington election turned a non-effec- tive Unionist vete into an effective one. Mr. Bolton's "conversion" cost them two more votes. This brings the majority down to 26. Then there is the "turn over' of the nine Parnellite votes, reducing it to eight on a division. But there is a seat vacant at Cork; Mr. O'Driscoll is absent. at the Antipodes; Mr. Coldwells has just had a true hill found against him in the Liberator ease, and Mr. Hardie is absent and "doubtful." This makes the majority four; but it is not certain that even this is an effective majority. Sir T. Fry is in Italy, ill; Mr. Jacob Bright is, I extremely regret to ,ay,lying at home seriously indisposed; and two or three others of the supporters of the Government are in such estate of health that their attendance is not to be relied on. No doubt there are. on the other hand, two or three members of the Opposition who are absent from similar causes, but in eases of this kind the Opposition Whips have always the ad- vantage, as they can choose their own time for an ack.

ati Mr. Samuel Storey. M.P., has intimated to his supporters in Sunderland that it is not his intention to offer himself for re-election at the end of the pre- sent Parliament. It is quite probable, I believe, that Mr. Storey may retire before that time. He has not been in good health of late, and is anxious to be relieved of tl.e strain of Parliamentaty life. The evidence taken on Wednesday before the Committee inquiring into the import of prison-made goods entirely justified the rejection of Mr. Howard Vincent's claim to have the investigation closed before completion. Testimony has been given by brush, matting, and rush manufacturers from the Eastern counties, showing that they attribute but little importance to the competition of these foreign goods, especially when made by the unsatisfactory form of labour which obtains in prisons. They de- mare themselves quite prepared to fight and beat these imports in the open market by improved mnehinerv, and that without the artificial aid be- loved by Protectionists.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, J UNE 22. 1895. LADIES' LETTER.

Mir DzAnusx NELLIE, lam in love with the fashionable jackets. A very smart little coat may be made in silk, crepon, or serge. It has large revers with their points on the shoulders, and the basques are cut very I smartly. It opens in a V shape at the back, so I that the shirt shows, and there are revers there as in front. It can be worn over any kind of blouse, and the elastic broad belt, which is one of the season's novelties, can be worn with it to advan- tage. The skirt to wear with this is wide and fashionably cut, and the blouse, with its thick guipure embroidery, is a great improvement to the jacket, falling over the revers, which generally display some distinctive material. The sleeves are short; a great attempt is being made this year to follow the fashion in that respect, which has met with great favour in Paris. There were never more charming materials worn for blouses, or prettie,' colouring, and they find favour almost to the exclu- sion of all other bodices. This particular jacket takes away from the one dowdy point in blouses, the waistband where the skirt and bodice unite. It wants a good figure with the blass of skirt now worn not to present too much hip, or too little or too much slimness, or too much obesity: Women are wearing a prettily made bow in the centre of the back, and often at the collar behind, and for these shot ribbons are greatly in demand. Another pretty costume is a jacket and skirt of green Venetian cloth. The collar is faced with a bright shade of tan cloth. The double-breasted waistcoat is of pale blue drill, spotted with black. The sleeves are very full.

The short cape which reaches just to the elbow has quite superseded the longer wrap, and these fascinating little garments are certainly very smart. The great point at present is to have them finished off at the neck with full frills or ruffles of chiffon, lace, or black satin ribbon, with knots of flowers on either side. The prettiest frill of all is made of goffered black chiffon or soft mousseline de soie, and in some cases these continue down the fronts of the cape in waterfall frills. These black ruchings appear even on coloured cloth capes, and look particularly well on pale fawn or biscuit cloth, with pink roses peepin,/ out of the folds. Just now everything has to bed la Henri Qua,tre, and I don't think I have described a cape made in this style. They are very elaborate-looking gar- ments. The one I refer to is made in black velvet. and the fronts and revers are of cream satin covered with Alengon print. The collar is wide and is made of satin ribbon, with bows and violets The cape is of the length I just mentioned as being fashionable, namely, just to the elbows. A suitable hat to wear with this cape would be one with a brim of cream guipure tabs resting on kilted black chiffon. There were also blacks tips and ospreys.

Some capital boating gowns are being made in cornflower-blue linen, which is used for the skirt and jacket. The blouse is of pale primrose silk, and a scarf for the waist and the smaller one for the neck of dark blue silk. The blouse is made on a thin, fitted lining, with the centre pleat put on separately. The turndown collar is made on stiff union, and mounted on a straight band. The jacket is quite plain, without revers of any kind, aud is intended for wear with the fashionable collars of lawn or cambric, which are tacked inside the jacket, and have the effect of large revers, with a collar at the back. This toilette could be varied by wearing a vest of palest pink silk and a collar of yellow lace and white cambric; or a white blouse, with the blue scarves and the camLric collar. Drills of any shade can be made to this same model, and form delightfully cool costumes for hot weather. A very pretty hat suitable for a little child, is made in ivory satin, faced with sky-blue satin, the full erownbeing :tily arranged with silk applique,

finished with ribbon rosettes and ostrich tips. It Is able for children from 2 to 10 years of age Is would also look very becoming in black satin, lined with colour to match costumes. Your loving JENrcrz.

The peasant women of Japan do not wear any form of head-dress.

Italy exported four hundred and eighty million dozens of eggs last year. An expert declares that eating beef regularly is bad for the temper. Lack of physical exercise has a tendency to make school children short-sighted. _ _ .

It is claimed that there are fifty-five dogs in the United Kingdom to every thousand inhabitants. The words " Emerald Isle," as applied to Ireland, were first used by Dr. Drennan in a poem entitled " Erie."

The Chinese Government levies a regular tax on beg.' gars, and gives them, in return, the privilege of begging in a certain district.

The letters in the various alphabets of the world vary from 12 to 202 in number. The Sandwich Islanders' alphabet has 12, the Tartarian, 202. Nearly every men, woman, and child in Egypt is a smoker of cigarettes, and a pipe is hardly ever seen in the mouth of a native.

The steamer Natchez, which lUDs between New Or- leans and Vicksburg, is officered by women. The cap- tain is Mrs. Tom Leathers.

Sparrows are very much like human beings. They have so much curiosity that they will gaze at their plumage in mirrors by the hour if not disturbed. A bat finds its way about without the assistance of its eyes. A blinded bat will avoid wires and obstructions as dexterously as though it could see perfectly. A closed room is bad for sleeping, because air once breathed parts with a sixth of its oxygen and contains an equivalent amount of carbonic acid gas ;air breathed six times will not support life. Piercing the flesh with even the finest needle hurts be- cause the nerves are so thickly matted just under the skin that not even the finest point can be introduced without wounding one or more. Miss Edith Rockfeller is one of the few American heiresses who have refrained from the choice of a hus- band of European birth and noble lineage. She has just become engaged to an American—to Harold Flower McCormick, son of the late Cyrus H. McCormick,the in- ventor of he reaping machine which bears his name. Great wealth is thus joined to wealth, for McCormick pare made an immense fortune in Chicago, and his id son, Harold, is still in the business.

Is Nose Bleeding Beneficial ? It is an old-woman's notion that nose-bleeding, se pre- valent at this season, is a very desirable symptom of health. Bleeding from the nose is caused by the conges- tion of the lining membrane of the nose, This conges- tion may be the result of catarrh, or, more properly, of the diseased condition of the nasal membranes which is due to catarrh ; or it may accompany congestion of some organ of the body, as the liver. It may also result from heart disease, or even from dyspepsia. Nose-bleed in children is commonly supposed to indic- ate nothing more than that the child tires easily or is over-active. Yet even these terms express more than is obvious upon a casual reading of the words. If a child tires easily, or, in other words, it the least over exertion at play or at study results in a more or less severe attack of nose-bleed, the child must he in a weakened state; while, on the other hand, if he is accustomed to allow his play or his studies so to absorb his interest as to make him forget his fatigue, he is placing an injudic- ious strai a upon his constitution. In either case he will be benefited by a curtailment of work and an increase in the amount of time allotted for rest, until his body is more fully developed. No child's mind can be developed faster than the body except at the expense of health.

Nose-bleed occurring in middle life and old age is a more serious thing, as it indicates a graver condition of affairs. It is usually coincident with diseases of the liver, heart or kidneys. Its cure is, of course, depend- ent upon the restoration of the organs to a normal con- dition. In elderly persons the disorder sometimes ap- pears to result from weakness, which in turn, it aggra- vates. It is a common saying that in full-blooded per- sons an occasional nose-bleed is beneficial ; and this may in a certain sense be true, inasmuch as the nasal int m- branes are thus relieved of congestion. But it is save to say that bleeding from the nose is never anything but a sign of weakness. The treatment of an attack of nose-bleed consists in absolute rest and cool epplicatinns ta the head. The extremities should be warm. The head should not he held down over a basin, as this favours the flow of blood, One mode of stopping an ordinary attack is for the person to stand erect, with the head in the usual upright position and the hands ex- tended at length directly over the head.

Cheap and Nasty. At the risk of the denunciation of my sex, says a lady writer, I must run a tilt against that woman's idol " cheapness." I don't want to condemn all eh, an things in a wholesale fashion. Against the cheap article whose price is the result of a genuine and permanent de- mand there is not a word to be said. May they increase and multiply ! But against the trashy goods with which the matket is constantly flooded, the "marvel- bus bargains," " ridiculous offers," the shoAdy shoes. " latest fashion," bad gloves " Paris cut," &c., &c. I must raise a protest. The cl•-mand for such is simply based on a perverted vanity and vitiated taste. Women are the greatest sinners in encouraging the cheap and nasty. Men, from being more accustomed to deal with gene ralities, do not err so much in this direelon. A man's first question is usually " Will it last ?" But a woman, larzely from her more circumscribed life, is ant to lock merely at the pleasure of the moment, and forth- with she will invest in some rubbishy article because it is " so cheap," regardless of the fact that it will soon be worn out, and so much money will have been wasted. It may be urged that the pretty cheap little fripperies are a boon to girls with small means in helping them to realise the natural desire to look nice. But this is really not the case. A girl buys a pair of shoes—the latest shape—at say 34. 11d. In a couple of weeks her feet look shabby. She ornaments herself in a cheap lace, which soon comes to pieces and looks drabby. Gin she be said to look nice ? The girl would look far nicer if she would expend a trifle more on a plainer shoe, and dispense with the cheap adornments ; or, if she must have adornments let her buy the materials and make some dainty thing with her own hands. She will then have a genuine delight in it for its own sake, and will have done something to raise the debased standard of art.

WE PARTED AT THE ALTAR; BY LAURA JEAN LIBBEY,

Author of "A Mad Betrothal," "lone," "Parted by Fate," "Floribel's Lover," etc., etc.

CHAPTER XXXVI. AT LAST. THERE had not been so much rejoicing at Thornton Villa for years as there was during the hour that followed Doris's return. _ _

It seemed a wonderful story to Mrs. Thornton and her daughters, that the famous beauty and heiress of whom they had heard so much, was indeed Doris—Frederick's bride.

_ "Oh, if you had but made a confidant of me and told me all, how different everything wquld have been," cried Trixy, laughing and crying in a breath as she threw her arms around Doris's neck, giving

her a real old-fashioned, girlish hug. "You are here just in time for Gwendolin's marriage," Trixy went on eagerly; "and a very brilliant idea has occurred to me in connection with it. We will have a double marriage. You shall be married over again to Frederick, and that will be your real wedding day. I will not hear a word on the subject in the way of objection," declared Trixy. "I must have my way about this.

"You will have barely time to rest an hour before luncheon,' Trixy went on, gaily. " Re- member who is coining at seven. And when he finds his dariing Doris is here—beneath this roof— he will not wait an instant until he sees you.

These lovers are always impatient creattires. Great goodness! how you are blushing, Doris. But there! I must run away. "Oh, dear I every one's love affair turns out happy but mine" murmured Trixy, with a little, half-suppressed sob. " Gwendolin and her lover are to marry, Doris and Frederick are to be happy at last. While Karl Lancaster, the young man I care for—Oh, dear, what a little goose I am for carin3 for you, a ;owl who doe* not dream of caring for me. But he does not know it and never shall. There's a great deal of consolation in that."

It was a very happy six o', lock dinner they sat down to; but Dori+ could not ea 5 a morsel. But no one pretend,: to notice it. She was glad to escape to her room. She quite fancied they must hear the tumultuous throbbings of her heart as the hands of the ebony and bronze clock on the mantel travelled shpwly—very slowly—toward the hour of seven.

Suddenly there was a quick, imperative tap on the door, accompanied by Trixy's shrill vozce, crying out, impatiently. "Open the door, Doris, please. I have something for you." Doris opened the door all in a flutter. Had Frederick come, and was Trixy bringing a message from him? But, no. There stood Trixy, with a pretty white mull dress, half smothered in lace flounces and knots of blue ribbon, on her arm.

"I'm going to convert you, in no time at all, into a veritable Cmderell k, my dear Doris,'' she cried. " As your trunks are not here yet, you mast accept the loan of one of my dresses for this ence. I want you to look ever so pretty in 'somebody's' eyes. And I know that somebody will think you perfectly charming in this." "Oh, how good you are dear," said Doris, happy tears shining in her due eyes. "Don't mention it, or you will confuse me," declared Trixy, laconically. "Come now, Doris. You haven't much time to spare, I can tell you. See, it wants a quarter to seven now." Then the work of transformation began. Doris was soon robed in the pretty white mull dress, and the pale blue sash knotted about the slender waist, with a cluster of blue bells, as blue as her eyes, on her breast. "There never was such a little fairy," Trixy declared, leading her up to the mirror. "True, you have not any diamonds on—and I haven't any to lend you; but who would miss such trifles, gazing at such a bewitching, blushing face, and eyes bright as stars Oh, won t every young fel- low around here envy Frederick, though ! ' "Please don't, Beatrix," cried Doris, blushing redder than ever. "I'm so happy, and you are trying to make me vain." At that moment the sound of carriage wheels was heard rolling up the avenue. "It's Frederick— at last 1" cried Trixy, waltz- ing out of the room, but calling back with a saucy little roguish laugh: "Expect to be called very hurriedly in five minutes time." And away she flew to allow Doris to recover her composure; but that was easier said than done. How her face flushed and paled as she heard that well-remembered voice in the corridor below— heard the tender, cheerful greeting that passed between him and the family. Then she knew, by the faint sound of their voices, they had taken Frederick into the drawing-room. How long would it be before they would send for her? At the thought a sudden, girlish hesitancy crept over her, and she felt like running away from Thornton Villa again. She went swittly down the corridor into the conservatory, but the perfume of the rare exotics seemed to stifle her, and she passed into the garden. How long she stood by the fountain she nev•T knew, but a few minutes seemed to elapse ' • then she heard Trixy calling her name; but she did not move—did not stir. All power to move or to speak seemed suddenly to have left her, she was so agitated. Then there was a confused murmur of Trixy had burst into the drawing-room with a white, startled face, declaring Doris had gone. A low cry fell from Frederick's lips, but before he could utter a word his mother put her jewelled hand on his shoulder. "I do not think there is any need for agitation, my dear boy," she said. "You must go into the conservatory and search for Doris, and take my word for it, you will find her; if not there, she will probably be in the rose-garden beyond, wait- ing for you." Frederick Thornton needed no second bidding; in a fia h he had gone from the room, and was hurrying toward the conservatory. He stood in the midst of the nodding blooms, glancing around him with eager, impatient scrutiny, calling, softly, " Doris 1 Doris 1" But no slight form pus ed aside the green leaves and sprang into his outstretched arms. "She is not here," he said to himself. Then he went on into the rose-garden beyond. The dusk had crept on, the golden s ars had come out, and a 3/oung moon was set like a jewel in the evening sky, shedding a soft, subdued light on the flowers and trees. He saw the glimmer of a white dress through the branches of the lilacs. The flush deepened on his handsome face. Some one was standing by the fountain—he knew it must be Doris. She was leaning over the fountain's brim, watch- ing the stars that were reflected in the rippling water, and thinking of Frederick—wondering how she would greet him—what words she would use. She longed for, yet, girl-like, half dreaded the all- important moment when it drew nigh. Down the lawn strode a tall figure, his heart in his eyes, as he watched the beautiful picture under the b ossom- ing boughs. He crossed over to where she stood, coming up behind her, his footsteps making no sound on the thick, green grass. He bent over her so closely that his lips almost touched her go den curls. Doris was looking dreamily over the roses, . won- dering why the moonlight falling upon them at that moment seemed to take a more golden hue; why the robins, singing their good-night songs in the branches of the beeches, seemed so joyous as they twit ered and hopped from branch to branch; and why her heart commenced to beat with a rapture so keen that it was almost pain. some subtle magnet caused her to turn her head —and then she saw him. Frederick Thornton was standing close beside her with extended arms, and & light on his handsome face that thrilled her to the heart's core. "Doris, my darling, he whispered, with tremu- lous eagerness. With a little quivering cry, she murmured: "Frederick! oh, Frederick !" and the next instant his arms enfolded her, just es they would hold her through life, pressed close to his throbbing heart, and love's passionate kisses were burning the fair, sweet face, the rosy mouth and rippling golden curls. "You know all, Frederick I My terrible mis- take?" she faltered. "Yes, all, my darling," he answered, taking her white hands and clasping them round his neck and firmly folding them there. " It was a terrible, yet a very natural mistake, which anyone would have made under the circumstances. We have been near being severed for life, my darling ; but thank Heaven, the past—is pas!. Vesterday at this time I thought I had lost you for ever, Doris, .. and life looked dark and uot worth living ; fur

What is life worth unless shared by the-One we His handsome lips drooped lower until they rested against the rosy, dimpled cheek. "lam going to claim the little bride whom fate drifted so strangely from my arms on our bridal day as soon as possible," he declared. "We both know how dangerous are delays. I shall claim you this very hour. You will consent if you love me, Doris. Tell me—do you, Doris?" A rosy flush surged from Doris's dainty white throat to her broad white brow for one little second, as she struggled to free herself once more from the strong clasp of those loving arms; and she shyly lifted those wondrous blue-bells of eyes to his face, with a glance so full of adoring love that it almost took his breath away for rapturous ecstasy, and he was answered. Then followed for the re-united lovers half an hour in paradise, as they sat together on the garden bench among the roses, talking over the past and exchanging eager vows of constancy and love for the future. "Oh, Frederick, we might have been happy long ago, if I had not thought you loved—Vivian, your first love better than me,' she murmured; "and I remembered, too, that you had wedded me on that fateful ni.ht of the ball, through pity's sake," she added, smiling. "Pity is akin to love, dear," he answered, tenderly: "It is not always the love kindled at first sight that lasts longest, dear. "I confess, when I married you, it is as you say —I did not love you, Doris. Shall I tell you when I did commence to care for you, dear 1" "Yes, tell me Frederick," she whispered. "It was on that weird bridal trip of ours, while we were on the train whirling toward Baltimore. You sat by the wind ,w, with your pretty face turned toward the outer darkness, looking so hope- less, so forlorn and friendless that my heart smote me for not trying to make you happier. "Then, again, the fall knowledge of my love for you burst upon my heart on the night you stood under these very same beech trees and confessed your love for the husband whom you supposed had cruelly deserted you, crying out you loved me, but would die to set me free that I might marry Vivian. On that night, when I thought I had lost you for ever, I knew the bitterest pangs of torture a man's heart can know. But we will talk of the dark past no more, love. Tho dark clouds have rolled away, and, thank Heaven' the sunshine of love has illumined our lives at last. "Come, dear, we will go into the house. They are sure to be tired of waiting for us." "I should think they were tired of waiting for you,' cried a saucy voice behind them. It was Trixy, who had come up just in time te hear that last remark.

"They haven't the patience of Job, please understand; and they've waited so long for you two that patience has long since ceased to be a virtue. Mamma has sent for you both. There will be p:enty of time for this billing and cooing in the future.' They followed Beatrix, laughingly, into the drawing-room, where the whole family was gathered to receive them. The girls would not listen to it that Frederick should claim his bride at once. It would be so romantic to do the wooing all over again, and have end in a grand wedding. Besides, that course, and that course only, would prevent that startling story of the past from being given to the world, and causing a nine-days' waisation, they declared. And, for the latter reason, impatient as he was to claim Doris, Frederick was forced to admit that plan was best. That afternoon a telegram was sent to the Lan- casters, iniorming them that Doris was at Thornton, Villa. "Of course, I am very glad to think Doris and Frederi k Thornton are to be happy at last," said Mrs. Lancaster that night to her husband. "But, oh! how sorry I am for our Karl ! Doris has wrecked his life! He loved her so well I He—he —told me so," she sobbed. "He is young ; tiine will cure all that. His heart is very susceptible. The next pretty girl he meets is quite liable to catch his heart in the rebound." "How can you imagine Karl so fickle as that?" cried his mother, warmly. "It is not fickleness; it is the way of men; human nature, any dear."

===== 0002601_18950622_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
than an Association which holds out to young men the light hand of fellowship and encouragvmt tit, with Chris- tian companionship, employment and recreation, going for to fill up the vacuum of their daily Hie, which shonl I be filled up for good and not for evil!' Mr. Reed waived to say before he concluded that he hoped this gathering would result in the young men of Warrington, especially those who were connected with the Y.M.O A. setting their faces against the great evils which they had to con- tend with in the town at the present time.. Of gambling he said it was most important that right-minded young men should see that this evil was very likely in the near future to do an immense damage to the young manhood and womanhood of the country, and he earne,tly hoped and trusted that there would be an earnest effort, made to stem this torrent of iniquity. Of drink and other evils he did not need to speak, but he turtler exhorted them to set their minds against the giant evils by which they were confronted, and he hoped that Mr. Crisfield would have the joy of knowing that out of that gather- ing they had been stirred up in the matter. The speaker then detailed the objects of the association saving of spiritual objects, that they were the great point upon which it was brought into existence and in the hope that their young men would be true to 'MAUR Christ. If an association was to succeed its objects must he kept in the front. He hoped that Christian men would rally round the Secr...tary and help by all the means in their power to carry out an important work in the town. Referring to intellectual objects the rev, gentleman said the E•Sill- dation had always placed before them the necessity of educating the young manhood. Thera were physical objects and of those they had an example o,ttside the tent. Of social objects they had a very beautiful illus- tration in the pleasant gathering that day. He hoped that Mr-Crosfield would feel encouraged,by the examples named in his hearing that day, to think he was only fol- lowing a real example and he had afforded a great bene- fit to the association by asking them to come that, afternoon. (Loud applause.) He (Mr. Crosfield) had welcomed them in a most cordial and genial way and , from his heart, the speaker had much p'easure in pro- posing that their very best thanks be given to him for his reception that afternoon. (Applause.) I Mr. S. B. STRINGER seconded the resolution in the ceur se of a humorous address. . _ _

The Rev. J. DAVIES in supporting the motion thanked the Chairman for the kind words he had ussrl in refer- ence to himseif, and he ?was sure that they had Mr. beartiest wishes and earnest prayer that good might attend the association in Warrington. (Applause, The Rev.W. J. KIRKLAND was very pleased to be there that day, and he most heartily endorsed what h.tii pre- viously been said. Ile was glad to find that their Young Men's Christian Association in Warrington was aCeoM- plishing such good work. Personally he was obliged to Mr. Crosfield and would be glad to put in an appearance 12 months hence. (Laughter.) The motion was then put to the assembly and carried With great enthusiasm. Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, who was applauded on rising. said they had heard one or two tales told list now, and they were really so excellent and so much to the point that he had some hesitation in bringing out a little tale of his own though it was more pleasant to give than receive. (Laughter.) The stories recalled to him a rather curious remark that was made by a relation of his. He did not know whether they recollected, but a few years ago there was a great rage for confessions—everyone was going about with a con- fession book containing a set of questions to be answered and one of those questions always was "What do you consider the greatest possible misery and the greatest happiness " and this eccentric relation of his, a maiden lady,answered in this way : " The greatest misery is to be in church without a pocket-handkerchief, and the greatest happiness to spend other people's money." (Loud laughter.) It seemed to him that his happiness on that occasion ought to be two-fold—(laughter)—be• cause he was dispensing what little hospitality he was able to offer on behalf of his pater who was away abroad (laughter), and so far as it was being given he was the giver and not the receiver. He supposed for that reason the cup of his felicity ought to be full, and he assured them be could drink that cup without the slightest ap- prehension of the consequences, because he kn^w that his father would be entirely in accord with all the ob- jects of a Society like theirs. (Hear, hear.) Esery clear-minded man must be in accord with those objects ' of the Society which had been so well ennmerated bv Mr. Reed, and he argued that the establishing of charac- ter was a primary object of the Society. (flear,hear.) It ought to have the support of everybody, quite irres- pective of creed, class or party—(hear, hear).—b ecause whatever might be the agencies towards which they looked to have the tone and level of the S -ic'ety main- tained, and even raised in this country, reformers had always to bear in mind one thing, and that was that by those agencies character and will formed the blocks upon which they had to build the social structure •, or in so far as modern civilisation might he called a thine of natural growth, the simile would be more accurate if they described it as a tree which they might trim and prune by artificial means indeed, but of which character was tha root, and will and energy the sap of life. That appeared to him to be the first and chief reason why that society should command support on all sides in their town and shoulu continue to flourish sad increase. (Applause.) They did not appeal to beer and skittles for support in that Society. Personally he was not an seattie- tic, and he was net going to pose as one either,latit what. he for one and everyone must feel,was that thee wanted to have a more intelligent and widespread IMP of the op- portunities that were afforded now-a.days. Their op- portunities for rational occupation and recreation had been enormously increased during the last generation.so that it was hardly an exaggeration when a man wrote the other day to the effect that they had these things laid down at their own door like gas and water. It was these opportunities for making men happier,and happier in better ways and in ways that would not produce the feelings of satiety, that they would like to see extended, partly on that account and partly because like those great friendly societies in their other spheres, the Y.M.C.A. was one of the finest evidences of co-operation in the best sense of the word they had in this conntry. It was one of the most encouraging signs for their civic life in the future, and for these reasons he had the great- est possible pleasure in doing what little he could, even if it were done in a left-handed way, on behalf of his father, who was away, in supporting the institution that afternoon. (Applause,) He thanked those at the house and those in the town who had helped him to make their little arrangements for the afternoon; their indefat- igable secretary—(hear, hear)—the ladies—who ought ti have been first—(applause) and the artistic faculty of the town were represented on that occasion (hear, hear) and he could assure Mr. Birtles and Mr. Pearson that I they were proud to have them amongst them, as the

town owed not a little to their enterprize and the power they had displayed in their respective spheres. (Ap- plause.) He wished them success, and with r.sgard to society he hoped they would find that their numbers increased as they met every year, and that it obtained the success which he was sure on its merits it was thoroughly entitled to. (Lnud applause.) Before sepaeating, Mr. Stewart (general secretary) announced that he had aonlogies for non-atteedance from the Mayor,Mr. Charlton Parr, J.P.,C.C.,D.L .(president), Mr, John Fairclough, Mr. R. E. Garnett, ane several others. Mr. Cro=lield, he add, had drawn their attention to one important fact which he would try to emphasise and that was that he hoped they would have an increased number of members. They had laid them. selves out tor increasi .g the membership by one hundred before the end of December this year. They were all indebted to Mr. Crosfield that day and there was one practical form in which they could demonstrate it and that was by increasing their membership. If they had as moth as 54. they could become an honor- ary member—the best advertisement they could have. (Hear, hear.) This, he added, was by no means the last of such nuts during 'he summer. The R .v. JOHN KIRTLAW, would not like to go away without expressing his appreciation of the efforts to entertain them of Mr. Pearson and his band. (Hear. ' hear). It was a great pleasure to him to he present and to listen to the music they had given them (Applause.) He moved that a vote of thanks be accorded them for their services.. Mr. A. H. Crosfield seconded and the mrstien was passed unanimously. Mr. CRO FIRLD begged to endorse all that had been said with regard to the music they had had. They had games of tennis, bowls, and croquet, and if he could have had a golf I link they should have had golf too. (Laughter). He indulged a little himself, so far as he dare, and he sometimes saw the anxious eyes of Mr. Rims watching him as the turf was cut. Evidently he considered that the game was by no means worth the damage to the grass. The speaker hoped they wou'A find the rest of the games sufficient to keep them going. (Hear, hear.) The remaining two hours or so were spent in a con- tinuation of the games detailed, and a great amount of enjoyment was aff irded everyone. Soon after 8 p.m. the party bade farewell to their genial and generous host, and artived home very much benefited, physically and morally, by the excursion.

TnE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, PINE 22, 1895.

A VALUABLE SUGGESTION.

HOW LADIES CAN OBTAIN STYLISH GARMENTS WHICH COMBINE GRACE AND BEAUTY WITH ECONOMY.

If ladies want to save money in dresses we advise them to obtain the material at first cost from the Provi- dence Mills Spinning Company and become their own dressmakers. At any rate if a lady cannot make her own costumes she can economise by saving middleman's profits on the material, which amount to threepence or more in each shillingsworth. The above mills supply readers of this paper with dress materials in the newest patterns • fast colours ; strong make; firm cloth ; stylish effects and the latest fashion, suitable for any age. In th.-se days of scarcity of cash it is interesting to know the way to buy the necessities of life d int from the manufacturers. We confidently recommend you to write to-day for patterns, which will be sent free of charge by 1 sending a postcard with your name and address upon it. These must be returned at earliest convenience. Letters should be carefully addressed Providence Mills Spinning Co., C. Dena, tment, Bradford. Yorks. N. B —lf you like they will send you some beautiful samples of knitting, embroidery, and fancy wools for home knitting which may be kept isy you. Gent's sum- mer suitings are also supplied at prices very much below any retail shop.

'SUICIDE OF A. WARRINGTON JOINER. An inquest was held at the Horse and Jockey, on Wednesday, by Mr. S. Brighouse, upon the body of Samuel Powell, 51. years of age, a master joiner,residing at 189 Llng.ford-street, who committed suicide at his workshop, 255 Winwick-street, on Monday afternoon,by drinking a quantity of carbolic acid. The widow stated that her late husband had had to undergo several painful operations some years ago, and recently there had been symptoms that other operations would he necessary. 'lbis had preyed upon his mind, and he had given way to drink during the last week or two. When he left home on Monday morning there was nothing in his manner to rouse any suspicion in her mind.

..... Emma Powell, 12 rears of age, and daughter of the deceased, said that at 1 30 on Monday, on her way to school, she called at her father's workshop. The door was locked and though she knocked she got no answer. She thee went round to the office window, and saw her father lying upon the bench. He asked who was there, to which she replied "It's only me dada." He told her to go home but she went to school, and on her way home at a quarter past five, when school was over, she called again. She then found the shop door and office door wide open. Her father was lying face downwards, his legs being in the shop and his head in the office. Thomas Perks said that he was called In by the last witness and found the deceased in the position described by the daughter. The deceased smelled strongly of carbolic acid. The bottle produced was upon the bench. Every effort was madi, to restore animation but in vain. Witness knew deceased, but had observed nothing peen- liar until the last week or two. He had not been very well, and a lot of work had come in which he had not been able to attend to. . _ .

E. H. Tapper stated that the deceased had bought threepennvworth of carbolic acid on Monday afternoon at about three o'clock. He appeared to be al: right at that time.

The pry returned -a verdict that the deceased had committed suicide during temporary insanity.

TRUE HISTORY OF MR. CLARKE.

I found Mr. William Clarice at his house, 32, Oakfleld- street, East Ham. His appearance surprised me, for I was not prepared to find a man with a bright, cheerful eye, a firm, clear skin, and the rosy glow of health in his 'ace. The traces of past suffering could be seen, it is true, but he had the air of a man with an appetite for the pleasures of existence. When I mentioned the ob- ject of my visit, he said " I could not easily exagger- ate what I have undergone from influenza and rheuma- tic fever, or the wonderful nature of my cure. I hadn't been well for over four years. I had influenza and my illness started from that. It kept me at home thirteen weeks, and even when I could rise I was not myself at all. I had a tired feeling, and rheumatic pains in all my limba. Two years afterwards I had rheumatic fever ; a very skilful doctor attended,and he couldn't rid me of the effects. With the rheumatic fever I was at home sixteen weeks before I could go to work again ; • and when I dil go I still had pains in my head and chest, could scarcely eat anything, and could never di- gest my food. I was ill again all last winter, from the first week in November, and did nothing for eighteen weeks. When I started work I could only just manage to drag myself about. After the first two or three days I felt the pains com- ing on again, and I thought I should have to stop work. Then I saw in the" Morning Leader "an account of the cures by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and thought I would give them a trial. So I got a box from the chemist, and before I had finished it felt a great change. The heaviness in the morning was en- tirely gone ; I felt light and fresh, and my strength began to return. I could hardly contain my feelings at getting rid of the pain after such a longtime: everybody that knows me says," There's a change in you somehow ; don't know what has done it 1" I kept on taking the Pills until I had taken four boxes, and have never had an ache or a pain since. Nothing ever did me anything like the amount of goo 1 that I got from Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. I feel capital now !" Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are shown by this and other evidence equally well :authenticated to be an unfailing cure for rheumatism, neuralgia, paralysis locomoter ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache,and prostration ; also diseases of the blood, such as scrofula, chronic erysipelas, &c. They are a splendid tonic, and restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health a specific for all troubles peculiar to the female sex, and in men effect a radical cure of all cases arising from worry, overwork, or excess. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are sold by all chemists, or may be had direct from Dr. Williama' Medicine Corn pany, of 46, Holborn-viaduct, London. at 23. 9d. per box or six boxes for 13s. 91. They are never sold loose, and any substitutes so sold are useless ; the wooden box must be in a pink wrapper bearing the full name, Dr. • Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

ANARCHISTS AND THEIR VIEWS.

The London Anarchists have published a pamphlet which they distributed very extensively during their May-day parade. The " Freiheit." New York, gives a summary of the booklet, which contains sixteen octavo pages. What the Anarchists want cannot be clearly dis- cerned, and it is still more difficult to understand how they intend to convert the world into a Utopia suitable to their tastes. But it is very clear what they do not want. The " Freiheit's " extracts show that the Anar- chists have plainly set forth what institutions must be absolutely destroyed before the world can become per- fect. We quote as follows :—" The belief that there must be authority somewhere, and submission to au- thority, are at the root of all our misery. As a remedy we advise a struggle for life or death against all authority —physical authority, as embodied in the State, oe doe- trinery authority, the result of centuries of ignorance and euperstitioe, such as religion, patriotism, obedience to laws, belief in the usgulness of government, submis- sion to the wealthy and to those in office—in short, a struggle against all and every humbug designed to stupefy end enslave the working men. Patriotism and religion are sanctuaries and bulwarks of rascals ; religion is the greatest curse of the human race. " We do not share the views of those who believe that the State may be converted into a beneficent institution. The change would he as diffieult as to convert a wolf into a lamb. Nor do we believe in the centralistion of all production and consunnation, as aimed at by the Socialists. That would be nothing but the present State in a new form, with increased authority, a veritable monstrosity of tyranny and slavery. " What the Anarchists want is equal liberty for all. The talents and inelination of all men differ from each other. Ever:one knows hest what he can do and what he wants; laws and regulations only hamper,and forced labour is never pleasant In the state aimed at by the Anarchists, everyone will do the work that pleases him heat, and will satisfy his wants out of the common store as pleases him best. "We are called dvnamiters, asssssins, devils. But the men who call ne thus are the very ones who kill more people than all the Anarchists of all countries taken together. The men who revil,t us are those who murder the miners by colliery aceidents,who insure rotten ships, set fire to crowded buildings, and cause railroad collis- ions by overworking their employes."

The British Government derives £2,220.000 from land taxes, £4,440.000 from house taxes,and £9,000,0 00 from income taxes.

The hair of erimmals is allowed to grow three months before they are discharged.

THE MAYOR AND CORPORATION AT

CHURCH.

Holy Trinity Church, Warrington, was re-opened recently,atter its recent eenovation, and the service wa attended by the Mayor (Councillor James Fair:,lough) accompanied by the members and officille of the Cot. poration. There was the usual pracession from the Pown Hall to the church, the Vicar of which (the Rev- E. C. E. Carleton) is the Mayor's chaplain. The Borough Band, members of the Fire Brigade and the Borough Police force led the procession. Tne Mayor followed, wearing his chain of office, and accompanied by Alder- man Greenall, (the deputy-mayor), and the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle). There were also present Alder- man Francomb,Councillors R. T. Fairclough,Smethurst, Skelton, Pierpoint, Evans, Bennett,Shaw, Hill,Shillcock. Lee, Baird, Hepherd, Green and Beaman ; Messrs. John Fairclough,W.Owen,and J.W.Havward, borough magis- trates ; Mr. G. F. F. White, deputy maeistrates clerk ; I Messrs. J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), T. Longden, (borough surveyor),Stanley Wilson (assistant town clerk)s J.Sutcliffe (assistant surveyor),S.Hunt (chief constable), T. S. Smith (police surgeon), R. Young (cemetery registrar), W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), W. S. Howard (gas works cashier), and C. Madeley (librarian). Others present included :—Lieu. Fairclough, Inspector McAteer, R.S P.C.C., Messrs. A. Jolley, H. Toft. Dobson, Price, tize. Thc police were under the direction of the Chief Constable and Inspectors Spinks and Cox. The Fire Brigade were under Superintendent Turner. The service at the Church was largely attended, there being a crowded congregation. The members of the Council were accommodated with seats at the front. The service was couducted by the Rev. E. C. E. Carle- ton, vicar, and Mayor's chaplin, the lessons being read by Mr. H. J. Westbrook. The newly•appointed organist, Mr. Percy Bandies, presided at the organ. The preacher was the Rev. Canon Dodd, vicar of Stretton who chose as his text, Psalm xv 7. • " Whoso doeth these things shall fall." Aftcr allu ing to the pre- sence of the Mayor and speaking upon his duties, he said that they lived in an age wl- en all their institutions and establishments, of which many generations of Englishmen had been justly proud, were required to give an account of themselves, and explain and justify the reason for their existence. The centre of gravity of political power had gradually shifted its position lower and lower, till it was now discovered in the broad basis of the popular will, a position, in the opinion of many persons, the most stable that could be found. But a greatly lowered franchise had introduced a force with which the institutions of this country had rarely had seriously to reckon. For better or for worse they had created a democracy, and a democracy be it observed which differed in many essential respects from other democracies of either ancient or modern times. In England they had drifted into a democracy which gave the power of a vote to enormous numbers of the least educated class, and which was almost entirely de- void of safeguards. It was true they had nominal safe- guards in the Crown and the House of Lords. But the House of Lords, whenever it ventured to alter in a slight measure a decision of the Commons, called down upon itself the wrath and indignation of a section of the people, and was even threatened with ignominious , extinction, while the Crown was expected to vindicate I its title to a constitutional monarchy by endorsing with- I out question or demur the current sentiment of the day. It was not his purpose to argue upon the good er evil of these things, but simply to impress upon his hearers the peculiar condition of things, almost unexampled in the history of the world, and to recall once more an often forgotton truth that the vox populi is not invariably the vox Dei. The tendency of modern legislation had been to place 'power' almost entirely into the hands of these classes, and under the name of the masses the great and final appeal was to be made to them on all (peg- tions affecting the welfare of this vast empire A student f the times, therefore, could hardly fail to see that they were probably approaching periods of great upheaval and changes. Increasing knowledge, ever fresh conquests in every branch of science, ever extend- ing facilities for locomotion, combined with the shifting of political power rendered such changes almost inevit- able. The serious cpestion was should they result in failure or success? Should this great nation stand or fall ? Religion alone could secure to this great country a glorious and a happy future. "The fear of the Lard is the beginning of wisdom." She alone could curb the insolence of wealth, and hush the complaint of poverty. She alone could fairly adjust the conflicting claims of master and man. She alone could teach the strong to be merciful, and the weak to be patient. She alone could bind class to class and inspire an united nation with one unselfish endeavour for the furtherance of the common good. With a coat pure and irreproachable,with an aristo- cracy, mindful of duty, and true to the ancient motto of " noblesse oblige,' with statesmen, who disdained to give up to party what WAS meant for mankind, who cherished liberty too dearly to allow it to degenerate into license, with a church that planted unfaltering feet in the footsteps of its founder, large minded, laree trusted, sympathetic, just ; with councils and corpora- tions that sternly resisted every species of corruption and intrigue, and use their legitimate powers only for legitimate ends with all who were in authority exercis- ing that authority AS a God-given trust for which ac- count must be rendered, a nation that had these things and did these things should never fall.—ln the evening the Vicar was the preacher. The offertory in the morr- ing realised £l7.

FIRE INSURANCE COMPANIES AND LOCAL A U THO RITIES.

Thefire insurance companies are,says a London news- paper, getting into difficulties with various municipali- ties, and the action which they are at present adopting may lead to disagreeable results. For some reason or other the insurance companies have been springing additional charges on public bodies without due notice. They have also combined wi.h each other, so that when a public body asks tenders for insuring property, it re- ceives but one quotation, or rather many quotations which are all the same. This has been the experience of the County Council, the City of London Guardians, the Westminster Vestry, and other authorities. These land other bodies have resented the arbitrary raising of charges. The County Council has a committee consider- ing whether some system of mutual insurance for public property could not be adopted, and the Man- chester Corporation has, as a preliminary, resolved to find out how much it has been paying the insurance companies and how much it has received in damages during twenty years. The Corporation had received notice that the fire insurance premiums will be raised 100 per cent. All this means that the insurance com- panies are provoking municipal action.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT, MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. H. GARDNER, replying to a question said the British Gov- ernment would be represented at the conference for the protection of wild birds which was about to be held in Paris.

- - Sir J. KIINNAWAY interrogated the SECRETARY for Watt about the proposed presence of the band of the Cold- stream Guards at the celebration of Low Mass on the occasion of the laying of the foundation stone of the new Roman Catholic Cathedral at Westminster,and Mr. CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN informed him that as some poli- tical construction or some construction connected with ecclesiastical polemics had been put on the matter and a meaning given to it which was not intended, it was de- cided not to allow the band to be present. Mr. Simw-Lxvigvas brought in a Bill, which was read the first time, to exempt from the regulations imposed by the Locomotive Act locomotives not used for trac- tion purposes. The House again went into Committee on the Welsh Disestablishment Bill, and resumed consideration of clause 5, which defines private benefactions. 6everal amendments were defeated, and the Clause was added to the Bill just before midnight, and progress was re- ported.

In the House of Lords the second reading of the Market Gardeners' Compensation Bill was moved by the Earl of DUDLEY. The Bill was accepted on behalf of the Government by Lord Carrington, but the Marquis of SALISBURY, while supporting the principle of the Bill, suggested that in Committee some of its details might be amended. The Bill was read a second time. Lord CARRINGTON moved the second reading of a Bill giving the police more powers in dealing with stray and diseased dogs in towns. The Bill, after a brief dis- cussion, was read a second time. The Dupe of Artorr.r, gave notice that he would at an early date call attention to the system of money lending at present in practice in the United Kingdom, and ask the Government if they would consent to the issuing of a Royal Commission to I inquire into the evils arising out the system.

TUESDAY. , In the House of Commons on Tuesday evening Mr. ASQUITH, replying to Mr. J. REDMOND, said he had come to the conclusion that JOHN CURTIN' now under sentence of penal servitude for life, was no longer fit, on medical grounds, for prison discipline, and he would he liberated on license. Mr. ASQUITH was questioned bv Mr. CARVELL WILLIAMS about the refusal of a Bedfordshire clergyman to celebrate a marriage because the lady bad not been baptised, and stated that so far as he knew this action was without any legal warrant, and he had no doubt the law provided an adequate remedy for the aggrieved persons:

Sir W. LAWSON complained, by RI eans of a question, of the granting of a special license to a publican in the , neighbeurhood of Castle Howard on the occasion of a , recent temperance demonstration there. Mr. ASQUITH was unable to say whether all the legal formalities bad been complied with, butsaid he was informed that great inconvenience had arisen on former occasions from the crowded state of the inn in question and the difficulty experienced by the public in obtaining refreshments. The House again went into committee on the Welsh Church Bill, commencing consideration of clause 6,which provides for the disposition of the property of the Churc 1 by the Commissioners appointed under the bill. Mr. D. Tfloatas moved an amendment to provide that the tem- poralities of the Church should be vested in a National Council. The amendment was defeated by 192 to 179. Mr. JEBB moved an amendment to provide that not only the parish churches but also the four cathedrals should be vested in the Representative Body. Mr. ASQUITH regarded the point more as one of sentiment than any- thing else, and said—thousth he knew he was running counter to a considerable body of feeling in Wales and England—he was willing to make this concession. The amendment was then agreed to. Sir F. Poland. moved an amendment to vest the burial grounds in the Repre- sentative i3ody. Mr. ASQUITH offered tointroduce words treating disused burial grounds as appurtenances of the Church. The amendment was pressed,and on a division it was rejected by 169 to 149.

WEDNESDAY.

The House of Commons on Wednesday resumed the consideration of the Established Church (Wales) Bill. Amendments providing that all public chapels and chapels-of-ease, as well as cathedral churches, all school- houses belonging to cathedral churches, or used in con- nection therewith, together with any land occupied with such schoolhou es and all ecclesiastical residences,should be vested in the representative Church Body, were ac- cepted by the Government. A proposal to the effect that as the Government had left the churches in thehands of the representative Church Body, sufficient funds should also be left with that body to keep the churches in pro- per repair was rejected by 197 votes to 171. Sir John GORST moved to omit the sub-section of clause 6 which provided for the vesting of the burial grounds and glebes in local bodies. The amendment was rejected by 205 votes to 191. When progress was reported the Com- mittee were still on clause 6.

SERIOUS CHARGE OF EMBEZZLEMENT AGAINST A BRANCH MANAGER.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Wednesday morning, before Mr. John Fairclough (chairman), Alder- men Harrison and Wright, Frank Garrett was charged that he on the 22nd December, 1894, being in the service of John Barron, 55, Tibb-st., Manchester, did feloniously embezzle and steal £4 2s. and within six calender months thereof divers other sums to wit in January last £1 15. and on 13'h April £3. Mr. Percy Davies appeared for defendant and Mr. Browne prosecuted. Mr. Browne, in opening his side of the case said that Frank Garrett was the manager of the Warrington shop situate in Bridge-street and in the employ of Mr. John Harrop of Tibb.st. Manchester. Mr. darratt's duties were to manage the shop and sell goods either on the hire system or for cash, and collect monies and account for them weekly by sending slips containing all the monies received less expenses and to put in a Warrington bank the nett sum. On March 18th, Mr. Hemp thought fit to remove Mr. Garratt and put him in another branch of the business and in consequence a new manager came on the scene and discovered the discrepancies. They charged him on a warrant with having embezzled and stolen three separate sums and the ingenious way in which he bad gone about it deserved credit. In the first case the sum of £4 25., he arranged with Mr. Hawthernthwaite, who was about to purchase some furniture to pay him certain amounts until he got the furniture, Mr. Hawthornthwaite went on paying until be paid £4 2s.,without the goods being delivered. When goods were delivered amounting to over £22, Mr. Gar- rett said he would require the old book back and get a new one. Mr. Hawthorntliwaite refused, but Mr. Gar- rett said he would give him a paper because he had to send the book to Manchester. Therefore he made out an 1.0. U. dated Juno 16, 1894, but Mr. Browne was pre- pared to prove that this was given in March, 1895, and on the day he left Warrington. The words of the 1.0. U. were, "1 owe you £3 125., to be brought with book when settling.—F. GARRATT." Instead of accounting for the whole £4 2s. the defendant did nothina of the kind, but went and put 10s. in the new book ineDecem- ber, and they found that this had been accounted for, so that in the first case instead of £4 2e. it should be £3 12s. Mr. Browne's client instructed him to say that Garrett had no authority to du this and ought not to have done it. That sum had never this, accounted for. In the next case of £1 15a. he only accounted for 103. The third case of £3 was a very peculiar transaction in which he gave an extraordinary document. which said, " Received for money paid £3, /eh April 1895." Mr. Browne applied for a remand in order that Mr. Harrop might get up the case, and show if possible from different sources what the announte of these embezzle- ments were and he therefore proposed to ask for a remand until Friday. Mr. Davies said he was instructed te ask for a remand until Tuesday,and also to sav further that defendant had a perfect answer to each charge. What he had to complain of was, and he thought they would agree with him that it was a serious charge, but that man was arrested en Tuesday afternoon upon a warrant from information from a manager but of what position he did not know. If the man was innocent of the charges where had he any redress against a man who might have no position whatever, and he submitted that the proper course was that Mr. Harrop ought to have laid the information in order that if there was nothing wrong he could be come down upon for false imprisonnaent or malicious prosecution. A very strong hand had been taken in the matter. Mr. Garratt was promoted to Manchester and brought back without a single word, on a war- rant, and no inkling given to him at all, and then locked up. Therefore Mr. Davies submitted that if it was true Mr. Harrop should not blush in making his story and stand the racket if he had done wrong. What redress had they against Fawcett (the manager) " who might be a man of straw." On Mr. Browne's agreement the case was adjourned until Tuesday. Mr Davies applied for bail and said the defendant was at present living in Manchester but he promised to stay with Mrs. Williams, who lived in Bewsey-street, Warrington. Mr. Be iwne said he was instructed to oppose hail. Mr. Davies said it was very hard when his client had been four years in Warrington and promoted to Man- chester. -- Mr. Browne • We deny promotion. Mr. Davies: It is only a N.,- month's since Mr. Browne appeared for the defendant and styled him as one of the most respectable men in town. Alderman harrison said that so far as he could see it was the proper thing to grant bail. It was a first off- ence.

The magistrates ultimately granted bail in two sureties r £5O and himself in one of £lOO.

v AN incident which has lately 'l° Eli. happened in connection with the oitttio North Somerset Liberal Asso- *itly le tend to clear the political atmosphere 15Clit'n.,-eattl to the Local Veto Bill. Ssudents of kt"l will not need to be told that a "dead • Of verY persistent character has been made the Veto Bill and that the movement has ,;4se:l4 confined to Tories. Two or three London L,lt'vtll;?4PerB have not merely given the Bill a '1„ c(),,-AseWarm reception but have gone so far as introduction as a mistake in tactics. ,5.ci17a,764 said that the Liberal pirty is far from ‘ict' ‘44itel in its siipport and that for the Itill.:ll,Clent to appeal to the country with this cter,,,,,Lhe forefront of its prwzramme means an I.*k,rli)oeicling defeat. Naturally these ominous L• r have had some influence upon even izilc• , %ben, and especially up in those who beeo closely inch) ti fied with the temper- the party. Mr. SID wEY WARNER, • • .'er N irth Somerset, appears to be one "444 ,-;'llnetnbers. He has, or had, an " open littono With respect to the Veto Bill. Not feeling bI I..either one way or the other, but being mth tc{act in this matter strictly in accordance "I'afings of those who returned him to

Parliament, arrangements were made for calling together the General Council of the Liberal party in the constituency. The question to be put to this Council was, broadly,whether Mr. WARNER should support the Government on the Local Veto Bill or should oppose them. The Council is largely composed of working men—of artisans, miners and labourers—and as the liquor party in the constituency attached the utmost importance to this unofficial referendum the most desperate efforts were made to " nobble " the delegates. They were inundated with literature to show the enormities of the Local Veto Bill. But what was the result? When the meeting was held Mr. WARNER unfolded his doubts and difficulties; and asked for advice as to whether it was expe- dient to offend the brewers and publicans who had promised to remain neutral if he voted against the Bill. The answer he received was plain and emphatic. There might be doubts in London as to the value of the Local Veto Bill, but there was none in North Somerset. The Bill, said these representatives of the sturdy Liberalism of the constituency, involves a great Libe ral principle, and therefore it should be supported by a Liberal member. The re- solution that was passed with enthusiasm by a large meeting, there being only three dissen- tients,requested Mr. WARNER to support the Local Veto Bill as loyally as he had supported other measures of the Government, the meeting declar- ing that the Bill "is calculated to benefit the people, on whom it will confer rights now possessed by a few privileged persons." This vote of a typical Liberal Council is bound to tell strongly in favour of the Veto Bill. It will at all events clearly show that whatever may be said by London journals temperance reform is an essential part of the creed of provincial Liberals.

The Insurance of Children Bill which has aroused so much opposition from the insurance agents all over the country has been withdrawn by Sir Richard Webster, and insurance agents may now rest content that their agitation has been success- ful. Sir Richard Webster intends to bring in the Bill next session with certain modifications to con- ciliate the opposition. There is no doubt that the Bill has been framed with the best intentions but it is said to contain provisions which would weigh heavily against poor people. There is some force n the argument that people with small income s can not always afford to bury their children re- spectably if it were not for the club money, and this Bill would have deprived them of the right to insure until the child was twelve months old.

Frequenters of the Warrington County Court will be sorry to hear that Major Nicholson will no longer fill the position of registrar of that court, as he has decided to retire, and go to reside at Bath. He has held the post for 17 years, having succeeded his father, and it would be difficult to find a fairer and more painstaking official than he has been. Mr. Nicholson is a local magistrate, but he has not often taken his seat on the Bench,but on the few oc- casions that he has been there,he has shown that he can be 08 painstaking there as at the County Court. Suitable reference was made at the County Court last week to the retirement of Major Nicholson, and he clearly carries away with h in the good wishes of numerous friends. It is satisfactory to note that he will be succeeded as sole registrar bi Mr. J. C. Ridgway, who has already had great ex- perience in the work of that office.

AN OFFENDING JURYMAN AT WESTON.

POLICE COURT PROCEEDINGS.

At Runcorn Police Court, on Monday morning, before Mr. James Handley (chairman), and Mr. J. A. Norman, William Owen, landlord of the Prospect Inn, Weston, was summoned for having on the 11th June been drunk on his licensed premises. Defendant did not appear but was represented by Mr. A. Linaker, solicitor, Runcorn ; and Mr. Browne, solicitor, Warrington, al:meared on be- half of the owners of the house, Messrs. Greensll, Whit- ley and Company, Limited. P.C. Morris, stationed at Weston, stated that at about 10.45 on the 11th inst. he was in attendance at an in- quest at the Prospect Inn. He had previously warned the landlord of the inn to attend and serve as a jury- man, but owing to him being drunk at the inquest wit- ness had to substitute another man in his place. On be- ing turned out of the room, the defendant remained at the door and commenced to annoy those present. Wit- ness removed him but he again returned to the court room door and commenced shouting and knocked at the door. Witness again went to 1,111, ..rol told him that he should report him for being drunk on his own premises to which he replied, " I don't care what you do." The Chairman : You summoned him to attend the inquest the day before ?—Yes. By Supt. Atherton: He was discharged by the Coroner ?—Yes,—ln answer to questions put by Mr. Linaker witness stated that he believed the defen- dant had been ill for two menths. He looked after the 'corpse which was taken from the quarry and it was in a bad state of decomposition. 1 Mr. Linaker for the defence, stated that he wished to direct the attention of the magistrates to the fact that the defendant had been laid up for fully two months with fever. The corpse was taken from the quarry to the defendant's house and he assisted to put the body in the coffin and then had to take it out again 'for the pur- pose of a post-rnortem examination. It was then in a state of decomposition. On the day of the inquest the defendant bad had no food for something like two days and his wife gave him some brandy. This seemed to have got over his nervous system. He was sorry, and he (Mr. Linaker) begged to apologise for the defendant's conduct at the inquest. He thought he might in a case like that, where they had a man of kood character which the Sup- erintendent admitted and he believed also where the owners of the quarry had been less troubled with drunk- enness since the defendant took the house, ask the magistrates to dismiss the case upon payment of costs. —Mr. A. Browne stated that he represented the owners of the house and if the magistrates would permit him to address them he should be extremely obliged. The magistrates after consultation with the Clerk, said they could not hear Mr. Browne in that case. Taking all circumstances into consideration the Chairman said they were inclined to take a lenient view of the case, but at the same time they must hold defendant responsible for his conduct. The defendant had been ill and ought to have known the consequence of taking drink. Under the circumstances he would be fined ss. and costs and they hoped that it would be a warning to him.

THE EXAMINER SATURDAY, JUNE 22 1895

THIC RECENT DISTRESS AT RUNCORN.

A meeting of the Unemployed Committee was held at the Town Hall, Runcorn, on Wednesday evening, under the presidency of Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., C.C. There were also present Messrs. S. Pope, H. J. Grine, J. K. Digges, S. Yarwood and Leadstone. The balance sheet of the distress fund was submitted. It showed that the fund commenced with a sum of £l9 119. 81. in hand. The donations received amouLted to £1,277 64. 2d., and £1 129. 10d. was added for bank interest. It is worthy to note that Sir John T. Brunner's contribution to the fund amounted to £540. The expenditure was as fol- lows : For groceries, &c., £1.082 64. 61; printing, £lO 169. ; sundries, £l7 44. sd. ; and per.. Mr. Sill for We4ton £3O, leaving a balance in the hands of the bankers of £l5B 34. 3d. The balance sheet was adopted on the motion of Mr. Yarwood, seconded by Mr. Pope. A vote of thanks was passed by the Committee express- ing its obligation to the donors and subscribers. Mr. Taylor was thanked for his services as chairman, and the proceedings terminated._
