RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

VAGRANCY AT RUNCORN. At Runcorn Police Court on Tuesiay morning, James McEvoy was charged with vagrancy on the previous day. P.C. Gregory proved the case and the prisoner was committed to gaol for seven days.

===== 0002601_18950622_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
Tax ALKALI AcT.

The President of the Local Government Board, in pur- suance of the powers given by the Alkali Works Regula- tion Act, 1881, has appointed Mr. R. F. Carpenter, of Bent Terrace, Prestwich, inspector under the Act, to be the chief inspector. FORTHCOMING TEMPERANCR DEMONSTIUTION. As will be noticed from an advertisement in another column a united temperance demonstration in support of Local Option will be held in the Pziblic Hall,Runcorn, on Tuesday evening next. Several well-known temper- ance speakers will address the meeting including th Rev. Canon Hicks, M.A., of Manchester ; Rev. J. C. Watk in, of Liverpool ; and Mr. Thomas Hardy of Stoke-on- Trent.

SHIPPING AT RuNconie. .0? Work is still brisk at the Runcorn docks although the arrivals of vessels have been the case for a short time. With regard to the large sailing vessel " Sutherland- shire "which is lying at the lay-by, the work of unload- ing is still being proceeded with, and another week will elapse before the cargo has been discharged. The vessel will then proceed to the pontoon dock at Ellesmere Port for repairs. When these are completed the vessel will return to Runcorn lay-by and will receive a cargo of 2,000 tons of salt for Calcutta. ST. PAUL'S BIBLE CLASS, RUNCORN. At a well-attended meeting of this class on Sunday afternoon, Mr. G. Bellamy gave an excellent exposition of the lesson. The chair was occupied by Mr. B. Knive- ton. The musical portion of the service included a solo by Mr. John Leathwood, entitled" Throw out the life- line," and Messrs. El ward Worrall and John Leathwood sang as a duet," David and Goliath." The accompanist was Mr. William Cooke, and he contributed an organ solo.

PARLIAMENTARY AND GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE.-

Tits NEW HALL.

Mr. Imison presided at the meeting of the Parliamen- tary and General Purposes Committee on Monday evening. The trust deed of the new guild hall at Runcorn was submitted and approved of, with one alteration, that the hall should be named, " The Brunner Guild Hall." The committee nominated Sir J. I'. Brunner, Mr. Imison and Mr. Kirkham as the Council's repro- at ntatives on the committee for the ball. Mr. Posnett's motion for the appointment of overseers by the Council was brought forward, and it was decided by the Com- mittee to ask the Local Government Board for power to appoint overseers and assistant o verieer, and also to ask for pcwer to adopt some of the clauses of the Parish Council Act which were not at present possessed by the Council.

DEPARTURE OY A RUNCORN TEACHER FOR VANCOUVER. Miss Mainley, eldest daughter of Mr. J. Mainley, painter and decorator of Penketh, left Penketh on Thursday last to board the Dominion liner so. Vancouver at Liverpool en route to Vancouver, B.C. The vessel sailed for Montreal, where Miss Mainley will land, and will be met by her intended husband, Mr. W. T. Stein, chartered accountant (late of Edinburgh), and will be married immediately on arrival. Mr. Stein went out to California a few years ago, and has established a very successful business at Vancouver. It will be remem- bered that a short time ago Miss Mainley was the re- cipient of a present from the teachers and scholars at the Wesleyan schools, Runcorn, where she has been teacher for some time. A BEGGAR SENT TO PRISON. '

At Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday morning be- fore Mr. T. Handley, George Nicholls, who had oecupied lodgings in Friar-street, Runcorn, was charged with hav- ing :begged in Bold-stre.,t, the previous night.—P.C. Kendrick stated that at 10.15 on Tuesday night he saw the prisoner stop two women and ask them for money. He then went to a house in Bold-street and begged there. —lnspector Hicks deposed that the prisoner had been six times previously dealt with. The town was overrun with men, begging, and be was sorry to say that they were mostly Runcorn men. They had received a num- ber of complaints.—The prisoner was committed to Knutsford gaol for seven days.

THE ELECTION IN NEWTOWN WARD.

The election ZI;.-representative on the Runcorn Urban Council for Newtown Ward took place on Saturday. The candidates were Mr. Thomas Reynolds, general dealer, Shaw-st., and Mr. John Jones, of the London and North-Western Hotel. The polling station was at Shaw-street schools. Mr. E. Marshall was the presiding officer at No 1 booth, and Mr. Walter Jones was clerk, whilst the presiding officer at No 2 booth was Mr. Joseph Nickson who had Mr. C. R. Lake as clerk. There were 649 electors on the register but as will be seen from the figures given below only about 400 voted. Polling commenced at noon and continued quietly until , eight o'clock. Not much interest seemed to be taken in the contest aid there were few signs to show that an election was proceeding. The ballot boxes were crin- veyed to the Town Hall at eight o'clock, and the count- , . , ing was at once commenced by the returning officer, Mr. F. A. Lake and his staff of clerks. The work was com- pleted at 850 and the result was made known as follows Jones, 219; T. Reynolds 182 ; majority 37. There were three spoiled papers. A vote of thanks was passed to the returning officer and the officials on the motion of Mr. Jones seconded by Mr. Reynolds. THEFT FROM THE PERSON AT RUNCORN.

Before Mr. F. J. Norman and Mr. Thomas Handley,at Runcorn Police Court, on Tuesday morning, a charge of stealing 153. 6J. and a handkerchief, the goods of John Faulkner, was preferred against Michael Burke,—John Faulkner, labourer, deposed that he went to Farrell's lodging-house, Vicar-st., about three o'clock the previous afternoon, and he had then in his possession 153. 6d., which he wrapped up in the handkerchief produced. He went to sleep in the lodging-house and when he awakened he tound that his money and handkerchief had been taken from him. Before giving information to the police he told Mrs. Farrell of his loss. The prisoner Burke was in the kitchen when he lay down to sleep. On returning with Police Sergeant Lee to the house, Mrs. Farrell handed him two florins and two pennies, stating that when he went out for the police, Burke went out, and she heard money drop in the yard. She picked the money up.—Sergeant Lee stated that shortly before mid-night Constable Kendrick brought the prisoner into the police office, but he denied knowing anything about the money. On being asked ho A much money he had, he replied 31. fid., whereas on being searched 83. was found upon him. The handkerchief produced was also found in his possession. On being charged with the theft he replied that he was sorry be had not spent the lot.—P C. Kendrick gave evidence as to arresting the prisoner. He corroborated the previous witness's state- ment. The magistrates committed the prisoner to pri- son for one month hard labour. DEATH AND FUNERAL OF A RUNCORN BUILDER.

At the age of 45 years, Mr. G. H. Thomas, a coatrac- , tor, Irwell-lane, succumbed on Thursday last, to a long and painful illness. The funeral took place on Sunday afternoon in Runcorn Cemetery, and was witnessed by a large number of people. The deceased was connected with Halton-road Wesleyan Chapel and also took an active part in connection with the Sunday school at Mill Brow. He was greatly esteemed and respected by numerous friends. Re succeeded in forming a band at Mill Brow nearly 17 years ago, and many of the mem- bers of the Runcorn Volunteer band were at one time members of it. As a :nark of respect this band joined in the funeral procession and played the Dead March in Saul en route. In advance of the procession there walked Mr. Pope superintendent of Mill Brow Sunday School; Mr. Find- low, secretary ; Messrs. Welsby and Temperley, super- intendents of the infant school ; Messrs. W. Graham. W. Lea, F. J. Norman, J.P.,R. H. Posnett, G. E. Woodland, E. Austin, James Jolley, and Captain le Couteur ; and the following representatives of the Bricklayers' Asso- ciation :—J. Vernon (president), John Pennington (sectetarv), James Ella ms (treasurer), W. Ashley, F. Ashley, T. Smiddy, T. Rathbone, W. Joynson, T.Foster, R. Poole, J. Wilson, W. Herbert, G. Sefton, C. Boote, Nickson, C. Nickson, and W. Hunt. Then came the hearse, followed by two mourning coaches supplied by Mr. Charles Dutton. In the first rode Mrs. Thomas (widow), Daisy (daughter), Mrs. Thomas (mother), Mr. Alfred Thomas, and Mr. Fred Thomas. In the second carriage were Mrs. Friar, Mrs. Millar (sisters), Mrs. Roberts, and Mrs. Sarah Roberts (sisters-in-law). The first part of the service was conducted by the Ray. W. George, and the Rev. T. Brackenbury officiated at the grave side. Among the wreaths on his coffin were those from his brother and sister, Mr. and Mrs. F. Thomas, and from his sisters Annie and Emily. It also bore the deceased's cornet.

SERMON ON "POPERY AND RITUALISM" BY THR B. J.

SIMPSON.

At Bethesda Congregational Church, Runcorn, on I Sunday night, the pastor Rev. J. Simpson, preached to a large congregation on" Popery and Ritualism—a call to Protestants. The rev. gentleman based his discourse on Psalm 44, lv. Be said he had taken the subject up through a sense of duty, especially when they consi- dered the efforts that Rome was making at the present time in order to convert Protestant England to Roman- ism. After commenting upon the projected invasion of England'by the Spanish Armada, which he said, was made at the behest of the Pope, and in which the Eng- lish people through the storm which arose, gained a signal victory, the ley, gentleman said the Pope had approached England again in a more subtle manner. Three hundred years ago England was tested and she was again being tested. They required more courage than their forefathers who fought the Spanish Armada. They required spiritual courage. He believed that they as Protestant Nonconformists would have to bear the brunt of the attack of Rome and if it was to be hurled back it wquid be by the Nonconformists of England. They had a great history and he believed a grander ' future in store for them. Wnen they began to waver let them think of their forefathers who faced the flames and the stake for the faith which they had received from their forefathers. Let them be strong and valiant, and let them go forward i God's name.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRaPONDRNT.

The subject of direct communication by road be- tween Runcorn and that part of the township of Weston known as Weston Point has been one that has engaged the attention of local authorities for a great number of years. It has been introduced time after time and negotiations have been opened on ,several occasions with the landowners, but as yet. nothing tangible has resulted. The question was under discussion at the meeting of the Weston Parish Council a few days ago, when a letter was road from Mr. Marsn all, secretary to the Runcorn Council, in which he stated that the Highways and Buildings Committee desired the co-operation of the Weston Council in their rfforts to procure a road for vehicle and pedestrian traffic between the two points mentioned, There can be no doubt that the lack of such a road is a great inconvenience to the inhabitants of both places, and another point which should not be lost sight of is the great danger to pedestrians when making use of the towing path along the canal, which has been rightly stigmatised by the Coroner as a death trap. It would bea good stroke of business if arrangements could be made with the landowners to give the ground required for the proposed new road—it is perhaps too much to expect them to contribute towards the cost of making it. There seem to be strong reasons for the supposition that the landowners vill,in the long run, benefit in doing so in consequence of the land ad- joining the road becoming more valuable for build- ing purposes, and it is to be hoped that the present negotiations will be attended with success.

Residents in the townships adjoining Runcorn will be pleased to learn that the Runcorn Library Committee have decided to reduce the price for the loan of books to them from si.per annum to Is. This, of course, will have to be confirmed by the members of the Runcorn Council, but there is every prospect of its being agreed to. In villages, good public lbraries are seldom met with, and it may safely be said that the residents in the townships near Run- corn have been ill provided for in this respect. They certainly have been able to make use of the Runcorn library but with a prohibitive charge of 58. per annum it was hardly likely that they would largely avail themselves of it. The reduction pro- posed is a step in the right direction and an increased number of borrowers should be the result. With the spread of education the time may not be far distant when Parish Councils will so take advantage of the Library Act that the village litrary will be as familiar as that in the town.

The difficulties which have existed for so long regarding the ferry between Runcorn and Widnes may now be said to have been satisfactorily settled at the conference held in London on Tuesday. The Manchester Ship Canal Company have agreed to abolish the penny toll for crosing the canal, and the approaches to the ferry are to be put in a more satisfactory condition. These were the chief points at issue as regards Runcorn, and it will now, no doubt, be unnecessary to proceed with the pro- posed action at the next assizes. It seems that there are at present no approaches whatever to the ferry on the Widnes side, those which existed formerly having been removed. The London and North Western Railway Company denied their liability to defray any of the expense in the erec- tion of the new approaches, and it will thus be saddled upon the three remaining parties,namely, the Ship Canal Company and the ‘Vidnes and Run- corn ratepayers. Many may be inclined to ask why Runcorn should pay a share of the cost of erecting approaches at Whines? But it is evident that Runcorn people using the ferry would receive the benefit of a good landing place at Widnes, and if this is all the expense incurred by Runcorn in putting the ferry on a much better basis, we may congratulate ourselves upon the recult.

At the annual meeting of the Rtincrn Musical Society, last night, there was presented a most satisfactory report of the past seasln's work. Mr. Crossley the talented conductor of the society un- doubtedly deserves much of the credit. Under his regime the society has improved wonderfully. The number of members has increaqed ; the work of the society has advanced in excellence, and the adverse balance which hung like a chalk cloud over the Aociety has practically been liquidated, the society's indebtedness only amounting to Is. 10d.

TITE RUNCORN AND WIDNES FERRY.

CONFERENCE AT LONDON.

In accordance with arrangement a conference of the various parties interested in the ferry between Runcorn and Widnes was held- at the offices of the London and North Western Railway Company, Par- Jiament-street. London, on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. Mason, soliciter, and others were present on benalf of the Railway Company ; Mr.Hunter(ringineer)and Messrs. Cirnncly, Kershaw, and Company, solicitors, Manchester, for the Manchester Ship Canal Com- pany:the Mayor of Widnes (Mr. Sadler),Major War- ing,Mr. Shores,Mr. Oppenhei in (town clerk) and Mr. Sinclair (surveyor) on behalf of the Widnes Corpor- ation ; and Mr. Imison, (chairman of the Run- corn Urban District Council), Mr. P. Speakman J P. CO., Mr. R. H. Posnett, Mr. F. A. Lake (law clerk) and Mr. M. Barker (surveyor) represented the Run- corn Council. The grievances existing at Runcorn regarding the ferry were clearly laid before the conference and the Ship Canal Company , agreed to abolish the toll of one penny for crossing the canal, and will construct approaches for high or :ow level tides at Runcorn. ' With regard to the approa?.hes to the ferry at the Widnes side of the river, a long discusiion took place as to who was responsib'e for the destruction of those previously erected. The Railway Company denied any liability and ultimately an understand- ing was come to that suitable approaches should be erected, the cost to be defrayed by the Manchester Ship Canal Company, and the Widnes and Runcorn councils. An agreement is being drawn up, :which may be considered satisfactory to all parties, and subject to its being carried out, the action which was to have been heard at the forthcoming Assizes, is stayed. The proceedings lasted about two hours.

THE YORKSHIRE MURDER.

ARRES r OF HUDSON.

The expectation that Hudson would be run to earth in or about Birmingham has been real sed. A man answering his description, and giving the name of R. H. Hudson, called at a barber's at Coventry and ar- ranged for letters to be received for him there, this be- ing followed by the receipt on Monday of an envelope and blank sheet of paper posted in Birmingham. Tne envelope was doubtless posted as a device to lead the police to look at some place other than Birmingham. On Wednesday evening it was made known to the Wor- cestershire police that a man answei ing his description had taken lodgings in Dawlish-road, Stlley Oak, a Bir- mingham suburb, and Sergeant Holmes waited his ar- rival there, and arrested him. He at first denied his identity, but ultimately admitted that he was Robert Heseltine Hudson. When arrested be had several cart- ridges in his pocket, and a six-chambered revolver was among his effects in a bag. Hudson was brought before the magistrates at King's Heath on Thursday, and was formally handed over to the police of the district in which the crime was corn- mitted. Inquiries made in Birmingham have led to the suggestion that the accused had grown tired of his wife, and that after he had killed her he replied to a number of matrimonial advertisements and inserted such an adver- tisement himself in a number of papers.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. Mr. S. B. Stringer presided at the usual meeting of the ahove,held in Wycliffe Church on Sunday atternoon,and spoke upon the work ot the P.S. A. and its results. The speaker was Mr. Johnson, of ?Agate, who,. gave a humorous and instructive address. Mrs. R. H. Pearson sang the afternoon's solo with charactt ristic skill, and the meeting was also taken part in by Meets. David Own, and G. Lowe. The president ot the movement, the Rev. J. Yonge, was also present.

RUNCORN NURSING CL&SS

PRESENTATION OF CER TIFICATES AND PRIZES. In the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, on Wed- nesday afternoon, Mr. F Wiswall, C E., presented prizes and certificates to the members of the Runcorn Nursing Class who had been awarded them for success at the recent examination. At the outset of the proceedings, Mr. Marshall, secretary to the Runcorn Urban Council, read a telegram from Mr.P. Speakman, J.P., C.C.,apolo- gising for his non-attendance. Mr. Wiswall said he need hardly say that it was very unfortunate that Mr. Speakman was unable to he pre sent, because that gentleman had had more to do with the nursing classes than he had, and knew more about them. Be was sure it was very encouraging to find a busy man like Mr. Speakman interesting himself with the noble cau-s which was the subject of that meeting. Mr. Speakman, Mr, Norman, and others were deeply interested in the m ttter, and when it was found that gentlemen like them were prepared to devote their time and energies to the furtherance of that effort, it was very encouraging. He knew of no accomplishment for ' women which was more worthy of commendation than that of nursing the sick. It was one of the noblest of causes, for by it they could increase the sum of human happiness. It had been the cus- tom of mankind to be at strife and to cause suffering and misery instead of alleviating it. If more could be done in this latter direction they might ultimately succeed in bringing mankind into closer COM- ' rnunion and do away,to a great extent, with the horrors of war. It was very pleasant to see ladies trying to at- tain this knowledge of nursing and to find that they were not only prepared to attend to the sufferings of I their own kindred and in their own houses, but also to , the sufferings of others. In asking him as chairman of the Technical Instruction Committee to present those prizes Mr. Speakman had that institution in mind. The speaker then proceeded to allude to the subjects to be I taught in the Technical Institute which he stated was to be opened next month. If they could find a sufficient number of ladies to form a nursing class the committee would be only too pleased to inaugurate one. They had ample capacity and, he thought, sufficient means. He was pleased to see that Mr.Lightfoot at the last meeting of the Council stated that it would be a good thing te es- tablish a higher grade school at the Technical Institute. He (the speaker) did not see why they could not and many thought it was practicable and should be accom- plished. He asked for their co-operation in making the Institute a success. Some pessimists in the town had stated that it was going to be " a white elephant," but he could say that it was going to be anything but that. After alluding to the small expense the Institute had been to the ratepayers, and to the material assistance received from the County Council, and the Science and Art Department, he said the subject of popular educa- tion, and particularly technical instruction, was coming more and more to the front. Their two County Council representatives, Mr. Spealrnin and Mr. Taylor, were both interested in education and were doing their best on that body for Runcorn. In conclusion he said he was only too pleased to be present that afternoon to do what little' he could in helping forward that good object. It was a pleasant duty to distribute those prizes which he , was sure had been very worthily obtained. (Applause.) Prizes and certificates for proficiency at the examina- tion were then handed to Mrs. Harrop, Mrs. Once and Miss Mack. Certificates were distributed to Mesdames F. R. Norman, W. Parkinson, P. Le Couteur, Whinyates, Rilcross, Littler, Davies. Matthews, and Misses Littler, Wilson, Rowlands, Smith and Withington. The prizes consisted of books on " Foods and Dietaries." Miss Mack proposed, and Mrs. Once seconded, a vote of thanks to Mr. Wiswall for presenting the awards. In putting the proposition to the vote, Mr. Marshall referred to the syllabus prepared for the Technical Institute. Classes would be formed for cookery, laundry workond dressmaking. The latter subject had been successfully taught previously. With regard to the cookery class, Miss Head had been engaged to conduct it, ant she had already highly distinguished herself at the Liverpool School of Cookery. Anyone who went amongst the poor would know hew little knowledge they possessed regard- ing cooking. There was a great deal of disease propagated through bad cooking, and it disseminated disease very rapidly. Ha would like to impress upon them to use their influence on the girls of the town and induce them to attend those classes. The Technical Instruction Com- mittee would need all the support they could obtain.— The motion was carried unanimously and Mr. Wiswall suitably responded.

PROPOSED REDUCED CHARGE TO OUTMDE BORROWERS.

The Chairman introduce I the subjcet of giving better advantages to borrowers of books, in outside district. He said any library authority could lend their books to any outside district either without or for payment. But it was scarcely fair that they should lend them without payment, and the general rule was that people residing in outside districts when taking advantage of a library [ elsewhere should pay more than an equivalent to the rates than those who lived in the district. Their Com- mittee had charged hitherto ss. per annum but that was I felt to be to much. For every householder in Runcorn the total mist of the library would not be more than 81. [ per year because the rate was levied on the whole assess- ment. In the country districts if they were to adopt the Library Act, which they could do if they wished, and join another library district, it would cost each house- holder about 81. per year.—Mr. Cunningham said he had mentioned the matter to several representative men in 'Talton and they rather seemed to favour the idea of allowing them the privilege of borrowing books from the Runcorn library at something like a shilling a year.— In answer to a question the librarian said he did not think they had above half-a-dozen outside borrowers,— Mr. Cunningham con%inuing said the more they could extend the usefulness of the library, the better far the people of the library itself. Some questions were asked by Mr. Pope as to the cost of rebinding books during the year, and from the answers given it was thought that one shilling per year would be a sufficient amount to charge borrowers outside the town.—Mr. Pope moved that persons residing outside Runcorn be allowed books on loan on payment in advance,of Is. per year, subject to a guarantee being obtained from a Run- corn ratepayer.—Mr. Cunningham seconded the motion. —Mr. Grim, thought it would be a great lboon to those living in the adjourning townships, and he believed they would be acting wisely in granting the privilege.— The motion was carried unanimously.

THE WIND AND THE GRASS.

I love the soft wind blowing Through long and waving grass; Though mingled thoughts and feelings, May o'er my spirit pass. Bringing the days of childhood, With heart so fresh and gay ; When hopes we fondly cherish, Too quickly pass away. Affections seared and faded, As withered leaves may be, Though love of holy nature Should live abidingly. I love to see the tall grass, Bend dancing to the wind ; In sunny scenes of beauty When all is glad and kind. Wavy grass and gentle wind, I love to watch you play ; And listen to your singing, Though I Icauw not what you say. But, oh, I love the music, In sunshine, cloud or rain ; Whatever ye are singing, How sweet the soothing strain! S. KENYON.

WESTON.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.

The Rev. Canon Wood presided at the meeting of the Council on Friday evening. There were also present: Rev. D. S. Bowen, Messrs. Tudor, T. T. Sill, Vose, Seeeatman Savage, Guest, Barrett, Ball, Clarke, J. Moores, and J. Cellier (clerk).—After the reading and adoption of the minutes, the Clerk read a communica- tion from the Clerk of the Runcorn Rural District Coun- cil stating that with regard to the letter received respecting the ashpits and roads, their Inspector had been requested to make a thorough examination of the ashpits in the township, and to report thereon. With reterence to South-road and Post Office-road, Weston Point, a Committee with the assistance of the Surveyor had been appointed to inquire into the subject.—The Chairman said he thought the matter was proceeding satisfactorily; no doubt something would be done.—The following letter from Mr. E. Marshall, secretary of the Runcorn Urban Council was read :—"The Highways and Buildings Comtnittee of the Runcorn Urban Council is about to open negotiations with landowners with a viow of inducing them to or en a road to Weston Point for vehicular and pedestrian traffic. They have ap- pointed a sub-committee to deal with this matter and I um directed to ask that your council will appoint a com- mittee or deputation to confer with my committee with regard to this matter. Your council of course is deeply interested in the obtaining of such road and we shall be glad to have your co-operation." The Rev. D. S. Bo wen thought it would be wise if they appointed a committee to confer with the committee of the Runcorn Council. From what he could gather the Runcorn people were hoping that the landowners would give the land for the proposed road. One road proposed was from Percival-lane, but he did not think that was feasible, it would be far better if brought through Picow Farm. They suffered greatly at Weston Point through not hav- ing direct communication with Runcorn. He moved that the Council appoint a deputation of three members to confer with the committee of the Runcorn Council with regard to the making of a directroad from Runcorn to Weston Point—Mr. Savage seconded the proposition. —Mr. Sill said it was rather difficult to discuss the matter as they had little information before them. He thought it would be better before appointing a committee, to write for information as to what land the road was to go through. He moved that the Clerk write to the secretary of the District Council asking for information as to the proposed route or routes, as to tiro estimated expenditure and as to whether the said expenditure would have to he borne by the ratepayers of the two townships or by the land- owners.—Mr. Tudor seconded.—Mr. Vose in supporting the amendment said the present as not the time for extra expenditure.—On being put to the vote the amendment was carried, only one member, Mr. Savaee, voting against it.—The following was moved by Mr. SW :—" That the resolution visaed at the last meeting. respecting the Clerk's salary be rescinded, and the fol- lowing be substituted : That in consideration of the extra work imposed upon the assistant overseer, by reason of his acting as Clerk to the Parish Council of Weston, his salary be increased from £6O to £72 a year, the increase to take effect from the 25th March. 1895, such salary to be paid every half year, on March 25 and I September 29 respectively ; and that the sum of £3 be paid to the assistant overseer for extra services in act- ing as Clerk to the said Parish Council from Decem- ber 31, 1894, the date upon which such Council came into office, to the 25sh day of March, 1895." Mr. Guest seconded, and it was unanimously adopted.— Mr. Ball drew the attention of the Council to the danger- ', oua state of a pit on the side of a footpath leading from IClifton to Weston. It was decided to communicate with the owner or tenant, drawing attention to it and asking I that it be put in a safe condition.—Mr. Guest referred to a letter written by Mr. Marshall to the Runcorn Rural ' District Council respecting the Weston water supply. He stated that the writer appeared to make out that Mr. Aaron had not been correct reapecting it. He thought that Council might support Mr. Aaron by the Clerk writing to the District Council, informing them the facts of the case. During the past four years the supply ti* a great number of houses in Weston had been intermittent and in some instances they had none at all during the day. They had a right to the water, for which they were made to pay. He moved that their Clerk write a letter to the Runcorn Rural District Council callingatten- tien to a considerable number of houses in Weston being short of a sufficient supply of water, and confirming Mr. Aaron's statements, arid that a copy thereof be for- warded to the Runcorn Urban District Ctuncil.—Mr. Basnett seconded.—Mr. Vose said his hens& was one of the highest in the township, and vet he had no grounds for complaint recently. He had been able to obtain water whenever he wanted it. The houses on a lower level therefore ought to have water.—Mr. Sill explained that there were two service mains supplying Weston,one running down Heath-road, from which there was nearly always a continual supply, and the other along Weston- road, in connection with %filch the supply was very defective. They owned 39 cottages, and it was very seldom they had a supply. These were fed from the Weston-road main. Thus it was apparent that the Urban District Council could remedy the detect by seeing to the pipes along that route.—Mr. Vose said that whenever the service pipes had been examined they received a full supply.—The meti, m was unanimously carried. A DomvsTlC SRRVANT'S ACTION FOR DASIAOI9.—In the Nisi Prius Court, etweer. on Tuesday. before Mr. Jus- tice Collins James Hull, domestic servant, Frodsham, sued the London and North-Western and the Great Western Railway Joint Companies for damages for per- sonal injuries. Mr. D. A. T. Cllt Williams was the Plaintiff and Mr. Bowen Rowlands, Q C., M.P., with Mr. E. H. Lloyd for the defendant companies. Plaintiff was a domestic servant.in the employment of a Mrs. Whitley, residing near HPIShV, Cheshire, and was about sixty years of age. On October 31st, 1893, she went for a day's shopping to Chester, and returned home by the 7.35 train for Warrington. On reaching Elelsby she was unable to open the carriage door or attract the attention of the railway officials, and was carried on to the next station. As the train was leaving Helsby the compart- ment in which plaintiff was travelling suddenly jerked or jumped, and she was pitched first from one side to the other and then to the floor, sustaining a severe shock which prostrated her.—The carriage window was also smashed by the accident, the nature of which had not been ascer- tained. Plaintiff was taken out at the next station (Prodsham) in an almost unconscious state, was sent back to Helsby, and taken home. Dr. Burton, of Prods- ham, and Dr. Butt, of Cheater, attended her and found her suffering from severe shock, from which she bad no. even yet fully recaveted. Plaintiff was not able to put her hand on any particular defect in the railway plant or working, but her contention was that an accident must have occurred to cause her injilry.—Evidence was given for the plaintiff, after which Mr. Bowen Rowlands, for the defence, characterised the case as a most myster- ious one. The Companies suggested that whathappened in the train might have been earned by rlaintiff's own excited and nervous action. Had the window been broken as he described, the shock must have been something tremendous, and other passengers must have felt it, but no other passenger had been inconvenienced. Railway officials were examined to show that the train and permanent way were in perfect order.—The jury found for the plaintiff, awarding her £2OO damages.

RUNCORN MUSICAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL MEETING.

_ In Bethesda Lecture Hall, on Thursday evening, the annual meeting of this B...eiety was held under the presi- dency of Mr. J. 13rundrit, J.P. There was a fair attend- ance of members and officials If tho THE SECRETARY'S REPORT.

The Secretary in his report for the Past season said that was the seventh season of the ikteiety's working. During the season three concerts were given as usual, which made a total of 20 concerts given by the society, I They had been honoured with the patronage of his Grace I the Duke of Westminster and Sir H. Brooke, Bart., and 'their patronage they hoped to maintain. Mr. Brundrit had again been their presidsnt and they had had 90 vice- presidents. The singing members of the society num- bered 85. A feature of the works performed by the society during the season was that each had been given for the first timG in Runcorn, and three works more di- verse in style it would be ORE ,ult to select. At the first concert Sullivan's "On Imre, on Sea " was given, and also a miscellaneous second part.. Mendelesohn'a " Walpurgis Night" was performed on the occasion of the second concert, and the final concert of the season was given in Lent, and consisted of a splendid performance of Spobr's " Last Judgment." He thought he might be permitted to report that at this latter performance the chorus surpassed anything they had previously done. In the refined, tuneful, and intel- ligent singing in this work was to be seen the fruits of Mr. Crossley's careful and assiduous training, and the value of his services to the society were never more apparent. The band, chorus, and principals all worked perfectly under their talented conductor, with a result that had been the universal praise ot al who attended the performance. During the past season three socials were held. They were of a very enjoyable nature, and added somewhat to the fund. of the society.- -Mr. Thomas Handley, J.P., moved the adoption of the report, and said he thought the last concert surpassed any other. The chorus seemed to rive every satisfaction to all those who heard it.—Mr. Kay seconded the motion, and it was carried unanimously. TREASURER'S STATISM, NT. Mr. Scott presented the financial statement of the society. It showed that there had been received from patrons, president, and vice-preshients the sum of £lOl 13s. ; members' subscriptions £l5 4a.; tickets sold £46 Ba. 6d. ; music, Itc., £54 ; making a total income of £217 51. Bd. The debit balarwe arid expenses amounted to £217 3s. 81. ; thus leaving a balance of is. 101. The working expenses of the year had not only been met, but a deficit with which they commenced had been liquidated.—Mr. A. Lightburn moved the ad( ption of the balance sheet.—Mr. W. Jones in seconding said it was of a very gratifying character considering their ex- periences during the last three or four years.—The Imotion was adopted.

BANKTIITPTCY OF A RUNCORN TINSMITH. At the Warrington Bankruptcy Court yesterday (Friday), before Mr. Registrar Ridgway, James Jolley, 14, Bridge-street, Runcorn, tinsmith, ap- peared to undergo his public exatuination in bank- ruptcy. The gross liabilities of the bankrupt were .£lO3 45. 4d., and the deficiency £95 44. 41. Vie alleged cause of failure, was bad trade and the expense of a large family, Its stated that he had been in business as a tinstnitn in Ruacarn for 14 ' years, and commenced with no capital. He had not filed the prescribed deficiency account. lie said that his deficiency arose from hi. profits being less than his expenses, as trade had been very bad the last two years. Be had seven children, six of them being under 13 years of age. He had kept no books of any kind, and had not at any time during the past three years made any - attempt to ascertain his pecuniary position.—After la few formal questions by Mr. A. B. Pot ter,assistant official receiver, the examination was closed.

MOST IMPORTANT.

Every person's future is, to a certain extent, in his own hand. Many a future which might have been brilliant has been darkened by lack of the spirit of self help. Many a home which might have been gladdened by life and health, and comfort, has been saddened by poverty, illness, and death, in many cases wholly pre- ventible. The spring and summer months are, from a sanitary point of view, among the most important of the year. Due care may now mean a healthy and happy summer and autumn, and robust constitution to meet the trials of next winter. Neglect may now seem a joy- ess summer, a trying autumn, and a broken constitu- tion to succumb under the attacks of any exceptional trial. There are indeed few that do not feel the system needs recruiting after the trying winter they have passed through. With same it is "only a little cough," and others," a tendency to bronchitis," with others, " no quite up to the mark," a sort of all over sinkingfeeling,l " too weak to do anything about the house," "these' recurring headaches," " no appetite for my food," kc. BEFORE TIM MAGISTRATES.

is. 3D. A LETTER. At the Warrington Borough Court en Monday before Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates, James Andrews, 25, Bostock-st., Stockton Heath, was charged with wil- fully damaging a seat in Bank Park, on June 4, by cut- jog his initials thereon. Park Keeper Ashton said that the defendant was cutting his initials upon one of the ornamental rustic seats in the Park. He went to him and got his name and address, and then reported the ' matter to the Town Clerk and Park Committee, who orderedlthe defendant to be prosecuted.—Mr. Monks : Is be so important that he requires his name cut in Bank Park. (Laughter.)—Mr. G. F. F. White (deputy magis- trate's clerk), informed the magistrates that the commit- tee did not wish to press the present case unduly, but wished to give warning to persons in the future.—Mr. Monks said that as the committee did not wish to press the case, they would take that recommendation into con- sideration. They, however, wished it to be distinctly understood that if similar cases arose in future, they would deal severely with them. The full penalty was 40s. and costs, but in this case the defendant would have to pay 2s. 6d. A BOGUS DESERTER SENT TO GOAL. At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Saturday morning, before Mr. J. Burgess and Mr. S. Wright, John Macdonald was charged with fraudulently pretending to be a deserter from Her Majesty's West Yorkshire Regi- ment, on Sunday.—Detective Sergeant Monaghan stated that at about 8 30 on Sunday evening he was in Butter- market-street in search of the prisoner on suspicion of stealing a shirt and he was arrested on that charge. When examined, nothing of an incriminating nature could be found upon him and he was told to go and went outside. About a quarter of an hour after prisoner came back and asked for his mate who was locked up for being a deserter, and said "I den% like to leave him, I am a deserter from the West Yorkshire." Prisoner was then locked up and on Saturday morning before Court he said he was not a deserter but had just come home from Dartmoor from doing three years for burglary. He , afterwards stated that he was not a deserter but had been dischatged (rain the army.—Detective-Inspector Spinks said he had interviewed the prisoner who said he was a ticket-of-leave man and reported himself a week ago at Manchester. His papers, he said, were at Man- chester, but he had not notified his intention of leaving that city when he came to Warrington. In Court the prisoner said that a fortnight the previous Thursday he reported himself in Manchester and the following day came to Warrington and then reported himself at Orford Barracks. He was not a deserter and was drunk at the time he made that statement.—Detective Monaghan said that defendant was quite sober and persisted in his state- ment though warned time after time.—He was committed to prison for 14 days' hard labour.

MINOR CASES. WARRINGTON BOROUGH COURT.

FRlDAY.—Before Alderman Harrison (chairman), Coun- cillor H. Roberta and Alderman Wright. 1 CLOVER STEALING.—A WARNING TO BABBITT OWNERS. —William Leigh (16), 19, Wackin-St.. and John Pember- ton (14), 28, Watkin-st., were charged with committing wilful damage to clover to the value of. 4s. and the pro- perty of Peter Norcott.—P.C. Turner stated that on the day in question he was in the neighbourhood of Manchester-road and saw the defendants in a clover field ' with the bundles of clover produced in Court. They were lying down in the field. Pe brought them down to the police station and got their names and addresses which he submitted to Mr. Norcott who took out sum- monses.—Mr. Norcott, Cemetery Farm, said the damage done was as stated and he had had a let of trouble through people stealing his clover, for some time.— A fine of 4s. 6d. including costs was imposed in each case. A PETTY SQUABBIE.—James Cliffe, 14, Foundry-street, was charged with using threats to Thomas George Morris. There was a cross-summons against Morris for ' using threats to Cliffe. Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins appeared for Cliffe and applied for a dismissal as the case was so paltry. Morris on his side refused to with- draw his charge and the evidence being heard, and a number of childish disclosures being made, the magis- trates eventually dismissed the charge, each side to pay its own costs. CRUELTY TO A. HORSE.—Frank Wilson, 68, New-road, was charged with working a horse while in an unfit con- dition on the 31st May ; William Warburton was charged , with causing it to be worked. Inspector Luff,R.S.P.C. A. said that at 8.30 on the evening of the 31st May he saw the animal drawing a cab and in charge of Wilson. Seeing that it was uneasy he examined it and found that its back was sore and inflamed. At his order the horse was taken out and had not been worked since.— Wilson did net appear. —The magistrates thought that the proprietor of the animal in cases of this sort should be held responsible and if possible summoned. As the Inspector had no evidence against Mrs. Kiernan, the • owner, the magistrates dealt leniently with defendants and fined each is. and costs. SATURDAY.—Before Mr. John Burgess and Mr. S. Wright DESERTION—WiIIiam Ernest Blakemore was re- manded to Strangeways gaol for being a deserter from Her Majesty's 21st Hussars stationed at Canterbury, since May.—P.C. Clarke proved the case and was granted the usual reward. !rams ArrasyaTioN.—John Connor was charged with making a false attestation to the 3rd South Lancashire . Militia by stating he was unmarried whereas be had a wife.—Sergeant Simon, Orford Barracks, stated that he . had charged the defendant with being a married man with children. He denied both charges. The magis- trates sent Connor to gaol for one month's hard labour, that being the minimum penalty. The sergeant was granted the usual reward. MONDAY.—Before Mr. F. Monks (chairman), Mr. J. W. APPLICATION FOR AMELIORATION OF MAINTENANCE ORDER.—Mr. W. H. J. At the end of that time if there was no improvement the boy would, they said, be immediately sent off.

THROwING A SToNE AT A SisTEß.—Charles Daniels was charged with having assaulted and beaten his sister,Mrs. Margaret Silcor-is, en the 15th June, in Penketh.—Mr. Longton, solicitor, prosecuted for the complaint, and said that his client at the present time was living with her parents at Penketh and apart from her husband.On Saturday night when complainant was about to retire for the night knocks were heard at the door. No answer being given to a query as to who was there,complainant called her brother Albert, and the two went to the bed- room window, from which they saw defendant and Mr. Silcock, complainant's husband. Some conversation en- sued and it culminated in defendant throwing a stone up at the window, which, after grazing the shoulder of Albert Daniels, struck his sister on the left temple caus- ing her to lose consciousness and bleed very much. Mr. Longton said that hit client wanted protection against defendant. —Albert Daniels corroborated, and Edwin Daniels who was called on behalf of defendant admitted that the latter threw the stone.—The Bench bound de- fendant over to keep the peace towards his sister for six months in his own surety of £2O and one other of £lO. COMPOSERS AND THEIR LITTLE WAYS.

Wilhelm Kienzl,the musical composer, is also a litt&a- teur. He has written under the above caption an in- teresting article which has just appeared in a recent number of the " Neuen Deutschen Rundschau." We can only reproduce a small portion of it, but this will suffice to judge Kienzl as a writer. He says : "To get into the right mood Sarti needed a large room lighted softly with but one lamp. Pier composed his largest works (Sargino, Achilleus) while talking to strangers quarelling with his wife, punishing his children, or con- tending with servants. Salieri walked through the most frequented quarters of the city with slate and pencil.to be able to note at once seme happy impression ; he was always chewing candy. Gluck, in order to translate himself to Tauris or Erebus, wanted the fresh air of the meadow, flooded in sunshine and sipping champagne. Cimarosa preferred to work while in joyous company surrounded by noise. In this way reputed his " tiecret Marriage," and this is a reminder of Mozart —wh.a wrote his exquisite E flat major trio for piano, clarinet, and viola in a bowl- ing alley during a lively game of ninepins ; hence the name Bowling Alley trio (liegelstatt trio.) by which the composition is often called—or of Schubert, who wrote the sketch of most of his dances and sons in the inn in the midst of a cheerful crowd. Paesiello composed only when in bed. According to Schindler, it was in the bath tub at early morn. where Beethoven re- ceived his highest inspirations. Zingarelli, who com- posed while sitting at the piano, always sought previous inspiration by reading some church work or Latin poet; after that his work progressed with giant strides. Haydn oat prietly in his chair thinking, but it was indispens- able that a ring given him by Frederick 11. was on his finger, so that his thoughts should become clear. Vari- ous are the accounts of how decided inspirations were begotten for the composition of some works. Carl Czerny reports that Beethoven received his musical idea for the scherzo of the Ninth Symphony from the chatter of the sparrows in the garden. Another version has it that Beethoven sat long into the night in the open air, when the thousands of glittering lights gave him the idea of the scherzo. It is told of Weber that he received his inspiration for the march in " Oberon "by seeing in a beer garden chairs piled one on the other ! Goldmark is said to have been impressed by the song of a lark at Carlsbad for the singular and bewitching call of Astaroth in the second act of his Queen of Sheba." These mat- ters are not to be laughed at. Extravagant as they seem they contain elements that make them important.

FRAUDS IN THE DISTRICT CAUTION.— Where imitation or mimicry goes no further than fair play, there is something to be said in its defence. It argues a certain humility of mind that a person should acknowledge himself incapable of originality, and it is better te follow humbly a beaten track than to do nothing. In some other ways, however, imitation is an unpardonable crime. Where some great discovery has benefited thousands of people, and is being sought after by all on the account, it is a cruel swindle for anyon- ignorant of the true discovery to foist upon unsuspecting people something resembling it in name or outward show, but worthless in comparison with the true inven- tion. Of courre the worse example of fraudulent imita tioo in this sense is the fabricating of pretended medl- eines, useless in themselves, on the strength of a super- ficial resemblance to a known remedy. such imitators literally juggle with lifeand death. This journal has, in common with all the newspapers of this part of the country, published during the last two years a number of remarkable cases, in which apparently dying persons have been cured by the dis- cevery published under the name of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and a large number of persons suffering from various disordors have sought the same remedy. Advantage has, unfortunately,, been taken o this demand to play upon the innocence of the sufferersf by offering a Substitute for Dr.Williams' Pink Pills. It is important that the futility of such imitations should be publicly known. Any retailer or detailer who:pretends to have "the same thing " as Dr. Williams' Pink Pills is trying to cheat the customer. Anyone who tries to sell Dr. Williams' Pink Pills loose or in bulk is trying to sell a worthless thing under false pre tences. The genuine Pills are never sold in that way. Anyone who says he can prepare the formula himself says what is not true: no one can possibly know what is no Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, or divine the new curative principle that they contain. Not long since a so-called analysis of Dr. Williams' was published—it was an ab- solute failure, and entirely failed to show the composi- tion of this important remedy. Another point is that " cheaper" substitutes for Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are a self-confessed fraud. There Is no subsitute at any price ; but in no event could Dr. Williams' formula be sold cheap. The composition does not admit of it. But the price at which Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are sold, and their extraordinary curative effects, make the cost very low indeed compared with other remedies or medical treatment.

As a final word the public should be warned against the statement sometimes made that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are identical with the well-known French Pbarma- copceia remedy, Bland's Pill. Anyone who says so manifests the most ludicrous ignorance Gf the very ele- ments of pharmacy, and the public will be well-advised not to run the risk of buying medicine in any form from a self-confessed incompetent. When any difficulty is experienced in obtaining Dr.Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, in wooden tube, with pink wrapper and the full name printed in red ink outside and in ; or wherever doubt is felt as to the genuineness of the goods supplied, the public are invited to apply direct to Dr. Williams' Medicine Co., 46, Holborn Viaduct, London, E.C., for supplies, which will be sent post free at 2s. 9d. a box,or six boxes for 13s. 9d.

EXTRAORDINARY TRAGEDY ON A YORKSHIRE MOOR.

A MOTHER AND CHILD MURDERED AND BURIED. On Thursday morning a double murder of the most de- liberate character came to light at Helmsley,near Thirsk, when the bodies of a Mrs. Hudson and her child were found buried on the moor about four miles from Helmsley.. The circumstances of the crime,so far as they have been elucidated, show that about three weeks ago a man named Robert Hudson, with his wife and child, obtained lodgings with Mrs. Holmes, at Bondgate, Helmsley, the former explaining that he wished to show his wife the natural beauties of the district, of which he was a native. The Hudsons, who were ap- parently on very friendly terms, took frequent drives over the moors, but Mrs. Hudson complained that the spots visited were very lonely. On Saturday, June 8, at nine o'clock in the morning, the Hudsons I left Bondgate, and the woman and child were not seen afterwards. Hudson himself returned later in the day and told Mrs. Holmes that his wife and child were going to remain with his aunt at Elovineham until Monday. He was going to join them at Hovinghatn, and they would return to Bond gate on Monday,on which day be invited Mrs. Holmes to j in them in a drive over the moors. Mrs. Holmes remarked that hehad better take the child's clothing with him, and he proceeded to the room to fetch it. When he had left the house it was found that he had not taken the garments, but had strewn his wife's and child's clothing about the apart- ment. On the Monday Mrs. Holmes and her daughter dressed themselves for the anticipated drive, but the Hudsons did not arrive. On Tuesday Mrs. Holmes received a letter signed "Mrs. Hudson," and written in pencil, requesting that her belongings should be for- warded to Darlington,as something had happened which prevented them returning to Helmsley. Mrs. Holmes fully believed that the letter was from Mrs. Hudson until Friday night last, when the deceased's sister from Sheffield arrived at Mrs. Holmes's house, bringing with her a letter written in lead pencil from Mr. Hudson in which he explained that his wife had gone away with a little money in her possession with a Helmsley man. Suspicion being aroused, the deceased's sister, a cousin, and Mrs. Holmes drove to Hovingham to see the aunt, who stated that the Hudsons had not been there, but they were expected to arrive on Saturday morning. The deceased's sister and cousin proceeded to Darlington and visited Mr. Hudson's parents, and were shown the clothing which Mrs. Holmes bad forwarded, and which had been washed and rut away. They could not, how- ever,get any information regarding the Hudsons. The excitement was great on the return of the sister and cousin to Helmsley, and several people determined on Saturday to make a search on the moors. Among them was a gamekeeper named Robert Baker. His search for a long time was futile, and he decided to go as far as Helmsley and get something to eat, and ascertain if any other person in the search party had made any discovery. About three miles from Helmsley he met three men, one of whom named Tyerma s told him that while repairing a road he sat down to take a meal at a certain part of the moor and saw IS hole dug under a clump of trees. only about twelve yards from the moor road. To the point thus indicated the search party lost no time in going, and on reaching the place they found that the hole had been filled up. A hooked stick was put into the ground, and the covering soil being only about three inches and a half deep the body of the baby was dragged out. The earth was again pierced with the stick, and one of the limbs of the mother was uncovered. It was then determined to communicate with the police, who on arriving unearthed the bodies and found a large carving- knife on the breast of the woman. Hudson is supposed to be about 23 years of age and Mrs. Fludson 25, while the child was about a year old. The throats of both the victims bad been cut, and the clothing of Mrs. Hudson bad been torn, as if there had been a struggle. The two bodies were taken to the Union Workhouse Mortuary at ' Helmsley to await an inquest,

TOWN COUNCIL NOTES.

There was an unusually large attendance of the public at the last meeting of the Warrington Town Council and this is generally a pretty good indication that something lively in the way of discussion may be expected. The rule held good in this instance for the meeting was both long and lively. For a time, however, matters progressed smoothly enough. The minutes of several com- mittees were passed with marked celerity, and the observations that were made were of a purely non- controversial nature. Councillor Bepherd called attention to the filthy condition of the disused portion of the river near Wilderspool but this subject did not receive the attention which the people who suffer from the nuisance no doubt think it deserves. Councillor Roberts put in a claim for better accommodation for ladies at the baths and some improvement was promised. After this the Chairman,of the Gas Committee,Councillor R.T.Fair- clough, dilated in a self-gratulatory way upon the reduction of twopence per thousand feet, but even with this sop the Committee did not quite !escape its usual meed of criticism. Both Councillors Smeth- urst and Plumpton wished to know how it was that the Committee would not serve out penny-in- the-slot meters to all applicants alike. Councillor Fairclough's answer to these questions amounted to this—that there were more applicants for these meters than the Committee could supply and they, therefore, let those people who were not at present consumers of gas have the meters in preference to those who had ordinary meters and who, he seemed to consider, should be content with them.

The minutes cf the next Committee—the Rivers Committee—led to rather unexpected develop- ments. The Committee proposed that a plot of land should be let by the Corporation to Messrs. W. A. Bishop and Co. for the purpose of constructing a wharf upon cocclitions which were duly set forth in the minutes, and which seemed to sufficiently safe- guard the interests of the Corporation. Councillor Hepherd, however, seemed to find something ob- jectionable in the whole transaction,and he moved that the proposal be rejected. His main reason for this action appeared to be that the wharf might in some way or other be prejudicial to the Ship Canal. This amendment met with a surprising amount of . support, though the arguments used by its supporters were singularly meagre and unconvincing. Some of the speakers seemed to vaguely entertain an idea that if the Corporation let this bit of ground it would be parting with a source of untold revenue. Councillor Monks, however, rather bluntly dis- abused the minds of the councillors of this notion, as clearly demonstrated that if the land was not let to Messrs. Bishops it would probably not be used for any purpose whatever. The Chairman of the Com- mittee, Councillor Roberts, warmly supported the proposed agreement and expressed the opinion that a little competition with the Ship Canal Company would be a God-send to Warrington. He contended that what was wanted in Warrington was more facilities for bringing material from Manchester and Liverpool by the river. !Nevertheless the amend- ment was carried by eighteen votes to seven. But even this did not end the troubles of the Rivers' Committee. The only other motion on its minutes was one appointing the Chairman the representative of the Corporation on the Upper Mersey Navigation Commission, and Councillor fiepherd displayed his opposition to this also. He argued that as Coun- cillor R. T. Fairclough had been the representative for the last three years he should continue to hold the office. He, therefore, moved that the minutes be referred back to the Committee. Councillor R. T. Fairclough at once gave the subject a rather personal turn by declaring that be objected to b.i- ing "kicked out". He had given up his position on the Rivers' Committee because of the dispute between his firm and that Of the Manchester Ship Canal Company, but he thought this should not debar him from being the representative on the Mersey Commission. Councillor Roberts denied that there was any "kicking out " about the affair, and pointed out that as Councillor Fairclough had been appointed representative when Chairman of the Committee so the Committee thought that now Councillor Roberts was the chairman he was the most suitable person to represent it on the Commission. Council- lor Monks and other members of the Committee supported this view of the matter, but the amend- ment was carried by twelve votes to nine. Coun- cillor Roberts then stated that under the circum- stances he should have no alternative but to retire from the Committee.

A long discussion next took place on the subject the recent fire at Messrs. Skelton's premises in Rorsemarket-street, and the part played thereat by the fire brigade. In answer to the questions put to him Alderman Hutchinson did not impart much information that was new. Alderman Francomb took up the charges that have been made against the Water Committee and the figures be enumerated seemed to prove pretty conclusively that there was plenty of water in the mains if it had been used. Another thing was quite evident from what he said, and that was that War- rington was not very well provided for in case of fire at the time the waterworks were taken over from the Waterworks Company. But,the Alderman added, diligent efforts had since been made to re- medy these defects. A little jocularity was im- parted into the discussion by Councillor Davies, who quizzed certain unnamed members of the Fire Engine Committee on their mis-timed activity and interference with the brigadeon the occasion of the fire. Alderman Sutton was judicial in tone but, "as an intelligent member of the Council" he offered his services to the Committee it was pro- posed to appoint to investigate the condition of the fire department, Councillor Woods protested against the meagre information vouchsafed by Alderman Hutchinson, and in the course of the subsequent discussion Councillors Skelton and Roberts severely commented upon the defects of the fire brigade ; Councillor Smethurst defended his action at the fire ; Councillor Beaman gave further information as to the supply of water: and Coun- cillor Monks expressed the opinion that the streets might have been kept clearer by the police at the time of the fire. In the end the motion proposed by Councillor Skelton that a committee be formed to consider" the gross incapability " of the fire bri- gade, and to recommend steps for its improvement war adopted unanimously. The remaining busi- ness was the proposal of Councillor Bennett with I regard to the width of the streets, and this proposal after some discussion was adopted.

THE WARRINGTON CORPORATION.

Oh, the Warrington Corporation Are a wonderful combination, They worry and fight, Despoil and blight The schemes of their own creation.

The Committees they duly elect To examine, approve, and reject, Then having done this With absolute bliss Their sanction they refuse to effect. That Town of a bygone age, Makes one shiver and shake with rage, For improvement are raze, With the Treasury bare, In that place without any sage. Can nothing be done to improve The men in the old, eld groove, Why don't they resign When others repine At the lack of any new move ? There's a host of things to be done, The money has first to be won ; The taxes reduced, The burdens reloosed, In good old Warrington. Then streets with much more room, Will in the future loom ; Whilst tramway cars On metal bars, The trade of the Town will boom.

THE GRIEVANCES OF PROPERTY OWNERS.

On II We nes lay evening last week, a public msetiug of owuers of property in Warrington and neighbourhood was held in the Cairo-st. s hoolloom. Alderman Harrison presided in the absence of Mr. A. Browne, who was unable to at- tend. There was a fair attendance,and on the platform in addition to the Chairman, there were Mr. G. Norton (president of the Liverpool Land and House Owners' Association), Mr. It. A. Bellwood, of Liverpool, Mr. R. Isherwood, Mr. J. Titchett, Mr. Stanley Ball (secretary). Among the audience there were Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. J. Harrison, J.l'. Jones, Winstauley, P. Edgar, W. Diggle, S. B. Stringer, Andrews, Dolan, Councillor Hill, and various other property owners. Alderman liaaarsom said be took the greatest interest in the success ot the movement, which he hoped would be instrumental in putting the owners of property in a more favourable light than they at present occupied. If the object of the meeting was to organise some scheme by which property could be preserved they would not have met in win. He was satisfied that there was a great amount of injustice done, and a great amount of wrong entailed upon innocent people of limited means, which wanted remedying, and he hoped the governing bodies of this town would not be long in recognising the strong claims of the property owners. Mr. NORTON, of Liverpool, president of a kindred in- stitution, said that his Society had been established over 35 years, and they had had a good deal of experience with reference to matters which interested all property owners. He had not prepared an address and did not propose to give one, but he would be glad indeed to give such observations er advice as they had gathered from experience. With reference to local authorities of which- there were several, from Corporations down to Parish Councils, he said there must, necessarily speaking, be a good deal of friction between these authorities and in- dividual property owners. Taking the question of an ordinary nuisance,the medical offiser stated to the magis- trates that it was a nuisance and it was perfectly use- less for anyone to say it was not a nuisance ; the magis- trates themselves really had not power to say anything else,but practically had to find them guilty of creating a nuisance. What their Ssciety tried to do was never to make unreasonable demands upon the Corporation, but when they had a real grievance and could make out a proper case they should fight it out, and the Corpora- tion would begin to respect an institution or association of that description. There were several things which he mentioned as serious and which would have to be taken up and looked into by these associations and that was the reason which made combination absolutely necessary. Every trade and business now had combina- tions and he was quite sure that there was no business or trade of any description in which combination was more necessary that in that of owning property,because they wanted taxation that did not simply tax landowners and property owners. He advised all to help in return- ing members of Parliament who would take a side on this question and who would do something in the next Parliament. There was a talk of a change of Govern- ment shortly, and if there was, politicians would go out ' on opposite sides' Out that did not matter to them as they did not touch party politics. All they wanted was fair play to themselves and it was right that they should secure the influence of would-be members in getting proper rating of all classes of property. This could easily be done by the principle of the income tax—that was to tax income from whatever source it was taken. Mr. R. A. Bellwood, of Liverpool, gave a number of facts about the work of the society in Liverpool. The rates of Warrington at 63. Bd. in the £ he considered rather high. In Liverpool, he said, by the efforts of their association they had been able to get the rates reduced by about sd. or 6d., in the L. He contended that abate- holders of Companies should contribute their share to the maintenance of the poor. In Warrington he be- lieved there was about 1000 property owners and he ob- served that everyone ought to be a member of the Pro- perty Owners' Association. As property owners they went in fairly for economy with efficiency and did not object to useful expenditure but only to extravagance. In some towns he had noticed even more activity than in Liverpool where they were getting attended to and any proper remonstrance they made taken due notice of. Alderman HARRISON said that he had just a little mis- understood the object of the meeticg, which had broadened out more than he expected. His impression was that the meeting had reference to the great number of houses there were uninhabited in Warrington at the present time, and in destroying which some people seemed to take a delight. He believed that the spolia- tiem going on at the present time was something terrible to contemplate, and he was under the impression that the feeling in the matter had culminated in this meeting so as to devise steps for counteracting the evil. That meeting had, however, dealt with the interests of pro- perty owners in various ways besides that of the annoy- ance and loss caused by mischievous persons. As the speakers had thrown out the hint it was really worth while for owners of property to form themselves into an association, such as they had done, for the purpose of disseminating their views, and gathering strength -3nd making themselves and their needs felt in the direction indicated by the previous speakers. 'The owners of pro- perty were, as such, entitled to as much consideration as anybody else. It struck him forcibly that the owners of property had not been fairly treated, and he was in- formed that in spite of the fact that there were police officers residing even a short distance from uninhabited property, that property was almest ruined. They should do something to stir up the feeling of those by whom they were governed in order that something might be done to put an end to the destruction. Mr. BELLWOOD said that with regard to the damage to property they had been taking considerable interest in that question, and in London it was almost as bad as in Liverpool. They had sent a memorial to the Home Secretary calling attention to the insufficient protection to uninhabited property in the country and asking for a more efficient guard. Alderman HARRISON observed that law afforded pro- perty-owners some pretection,but sometimes it happened that tee delinquents were not worth suing and they could not "get blood from a stone." In such cases "the remedy was worse than the disease." Mr. J. TITCHETT was pleased that they had a meeting to consider the cpiestion ot the interests of property- owners. The object of the meeting was that they should try to further the interests of the Warrington property- owners Association and it was considered some two months ago that a meeting of this kind might be the means of increasing their strength and so place them in a position to cope with the difficulties they had to con- tend with in Warrington. He himself had spent pounds and pounds to put property in repair and paid a private watchman, and the abuse he had received from the neighbours in the locality was out of all manner of reason. He thought it high time that something was done to put an end to that kind of thing. (Hear, hear.) With regard to street-making he thought that it came very heavy upon a man which had sa red a few hundred pounds out of his earnines,and built half-a-dozen houses, to be obliged to pay for the street which was used by the public. The speaker was of the opinion that the pay- ments for street-makiee ought to be spread over a large number of years, say 10 or 12, to give the man a fair change to meet his liabilities. A vote of thanks was passed to Messrs. Bellwood and Norton for their instructive addresses, and to the Chair- man for so ably presiding over the assembly.

A REMARKABLE I'ATERNITY CASE.

STRANGE DEVELOPMENT.

A remarkable development took place on Monday in the paternity case which has been before Mr. Justice Hawkins for several days. The reputed mother of the infant plaintiff, Mrs. Salisbury, claims that the child is her daughter, and that she is consequently entitled to property of considerable value left by its father. On Monday the evidence of Dr. Capon, which had been taken on commission owing to his illness, was read. He stated that Mrs. Salisbury sometime' after her husband's death asked him if he could find her a child, as she wished to adopt one. He put her in communication with the witness Wright, who had a child in February, 1894, and Wright's child was, he believed, handed over either to Mrs. Salisbury or her sister, Mrs. Pike. After this evidence had been put in, Mr. Jeff, QC., who appeared for Mrs. Salisbury, retired from the case. Mrs. Salisbury denied the truth of the statesments made by Dr. Capon, and asked for an adjournment. The Judge, after stating that the case must new be probed to the bottom, adjourned the trial until next month.

The Court of Inquiry into the collision between the Crathie and the Elbe met on Monday to deliver ju ig- ment. The Court held that the mate of the Crathie, Rollert H. Craig, was in default, and they cancelled his certificate. At the same time they intimated that the officer in charge of the Elbe might and ought to have stopped the engines when he saw there was danger of a collision. If be had done so the disaster could have been averted.
