THE REV. PETER MACKENZIE AT

WARRINGTON.

The Rev. Peter MacKenzie paid one of his periodical visits to Warrington on Sunday and Monday. On Sun- day be preached at the Bolc-street Wesleyan Church to large congregations, his discourse at night upon the " Prodigal son " being both edifying and humorous. On Monday evening, he delivered a lecture upen "Absalom, his privileges, perversities, and punish- ments." Ther3 was a fair attendance, and the chair was occupied by Councillor Davis of Widnes, and he was supported by the Revs. J. Kirtlan, Owen Watkins, and G. D. Mason. The Rev. J. KIRTLAN in introducing the Chairman, expressed his pleasure that a worker in another circuit took the chair on that occasion, and hoped that a more frequent intercourse between the neighbouring circuits would take place. The CHAIRMAN jn reciprocating Mr. Kirtlan's senti- ments, said that he thought that as the circuits of Warrington, Widnes, Runcorn and St. Helens were so near to each other a Methodist council would be a very desirable thing. As Warrington was the oldest circuit, he thought it should be the one to take the matter up, and there were many ways in which it would be made useful. As regarded the 'lecturer, he did not think that any man from John o' Groats to Lands End was better known, and although he (the chairman) had never prev- iously been the chairman at one of his lectures, he had often been much edified with them. Ile then asked the Lecturer to address the meeting. The Rev. PETER MacKsezie then proceeded with his lecture which was interspersed throughout with a vast amount of humour, and was a most characteristic address. He said that they could not find much pleasure and satisfaction in studying the character and history of Prince Absalom, as he was not like some of he biblical characters who were great, good and wise. His history was, however, written for their profit and edification. The privileges of the prince were many and great, and he had a glorious future in prospect. He was remark- able for his beauty. He only wished that Abealona had accomplished the possibilities of his chances. What could he not have done ? But Absalom had the ugliest heart that was ever born by anyone between this or any other place. He had the poorest and most wretched skunk of a soul that ever disgraced humanity or ever came under the influences of the evil one. The lecturer glen went on to describe in a vivid manner, the murder by Absalom of his elder brother Amnon, who had acted improperly with his sister Tamar two years previously. For this crime, he was obliged to flee to Geshur, of which place his grandfather was king. After three years'banishment, David, his father, allowed him to return, but refused to take him into favour for two more years, when he was forgiven everything, and treated as a prince. Notwithstanding his forgiveness, he had not a spark of gratitude towards his father. He commenced to have designs upon the throne, and began to ingratiate himself with the people. He pre- tended to have the welfare of the people at heart, and gave great promise of what he could do were he to be king. He also endeavoured to use religion as a cloak for his misdoing just as people did at the present time. He said that but for the pretence of religion, it would be impossible for many scoundrels to perform their wicked work. Did they but come out in their true colours as sons of the Devil, they would have a poor chance of gulling the people. (Applause.) After he thought the time for action had arrived, he pretended that he had made a vow to sacrifice to the Lord at Hebron, and asked permission of David to go there, and this was readily granted. Here with the heln of Ahithophel, he raised the standard of revolt, and David, not knowing who to trust in, took flight across he Jordan with a few trusty followers. The good advice of Ahithophel to pur- sue David while he had a few followers was over-ruled in favour of council by Flushai (who was a secret friend of David) to wait until a vast army could be raised. The lecturer then proceeded in a dramatic manner to des- cribe the battle which took place between Absalom and David's generals, resulting in a complete overthrow and death of Absalom. He then described the great anguish which David endured at the news of the death of his son. His first question after the battle was over, being "Is the young man Absalom safe " He said that there were many lessons to be derived from a study of Absa- lom' character. No young man would be ea fe who kept bad company. He would always suffer by being con- taminated with wicked young men. He would not be safe if he indulged in gambling, or strong drink. He would, however, be safe if he put his trust in Jesus Christ, and allowed Him to guide and direct his foot- steps. As long as he trusted In Him, he would never go wrong, and in conclusion, he ureed upon his hearers to be warned by the example of Absalom, and to keep on the right path. (Applause,) The meeting terminated in the usual manner.

===== 0002601_18950622_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING,

-- A meeting of the Parish Council was held at the school-room on Monday evening. In the absence of Mr. C. W. Davenport, Mr. F. Pearson presided, and there were also present—Messrs. Ashton Hayes, J. Whitlow, W. Coppock, Hugh Darhoshire, J Brirch, John Darby- shire, W. Richardson, and J. Ellison (clerk).—The Chair- man read a letter which he had received from Mr. E. England, 3, Carlton-terrace, Stockton Heath, upon the advisability of having a recreation ground at Stockton Reath. The letter was as follows Dear Sir,—l shall fee,obliged if your Council will take into considera- tion the question of acquiring a ground for the purpose of recreation for the inhabitants of the Stockton heath district. You and your colleagues may have already considered the question, thou7h I have not heard that you have done so, but yet I do know that there is a general feeling that a public recreation ground would be a great boon to the district. I need scarcely point out to you that as time goes on, it will he more difficult to obtain suitable land for the purpose, and only then at a greatly increased cost than could be obtained at the pre- sent time. There is a feeling that the present time is an opportune one, seeing that the rates in the district are very light,and if the eronnd could he obtained on a long lease the necessary rate required for the purpose ' would net he of a burdensome nature. I need scarcely add that if a pelelic recreation ground could he obtainod, that it could not fail tv attract more residents to the district, and consequently an increase in the rateable ' value,'which would help to recoup the outlay neeessary for the purpose.—Yours truly, E. ENGLAND." He (the Chairman) had looked round and had found a suitable' spot in the form of a field situated in a snot known as Springfield. The field was the property of Mr. Warbur- ton, and was about feur acres in extent.—Mr. Whitlow said he understood that the recreation ground would only be for Stockton Heath, but then,: were also plenty of children in Appleton who were running about, and who had nowhere to go to play and enjoy themselves.— The Chairman said he thought that every village inEnv- land ought to have a recreation groundond he thought that Appleton should have the same advantage as Stockton Heath.—Mr. Richardson proposed that the Clerk should write to Mr. Warburton concerning the matter.—The Chairman seconded.—Mr. Whitlow moved as an amendment that no further notice should he taken of the matter.—Mr. Britch seconded.—The motion was carried and this concluded the business of the meeting.

OILS 3N GAMLS AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICKET.

Warrington have varied their usual proceedings by obliging us with a win on Saturday, and not a narrow win either, for they beat Oldham by 169 to 62, and even with this large total, they had two wickets to spare. There is nothing like doing a thing properly when one gets a chance. This is Warrington's second win, and their record now stands at two wins, four losses, and two draws. It is however probable that their wins will remain stationary for the next two weeks tor they have to meet Preston and Liverpool both away from home,and as they could not manage these teams at Warrington, it can hardly bb expected that they will at Preston and Liverpool. However it is to be hoped that they will up- set these calculations.

The match on Saturday was a very enjoyable one—for Warrington. They were not favoured by the spin of the coin, and therefore they did not have the advantage of first batting. Oldham however could do nothing against the deliveries cf Connor and J. J. Crosfield, and six of the batsmen were clean bowled. The Warrington bow- lers had excellent figures, J. J. Crosfield's six wickets only costing 17, a most remarkable average, while Con- , nor's four only cost 35. Only one Oldhamer got into double figures and that was Sinkinson, who only made 10.

That it was net the state of the wicket which favoured the bowlers was quickly shown by Crisfield and Lieut. Plomer, the latter of whom is a decided acquisition to the Warrington team. The two batsmen hit out with such vigour that 50 runs were added within half an hour, and 55 had been scored before Plomer was dis- missed. Callon, who came to Crosfield's assistance, fol- lowed the example set by his partner, and he also hit out, and with the, loss of only one wicket, Oldham had been beaten. 100 runs had actually been scored for the loss of only two wickets. Callon was the top scorer of the match, his contribution being 39, and Plomer and Crosfield had 30 and 29 respectively, while Bates and George Clarke tied with 25 each. Only Wordsworth failed to score.

This victory of Warrington has roused the spirits of their followers, and it is to be hoped that they will con- tinue their course in a similar manner on Saturday.

The averages of the Warrington batsmen have con- siderably improved this week. Lieutenant Plomer is at the head of the averages with 22, but as he has only played in two matches, his name dues not appear in the list, and R. Smith still remains at the top. Bates has now passed his 100, and has scored exactly the same number of runs as Smith,viz.. 169. o:her batsmen are also getting within measureable reach of 100. J.J.Cros- field, who now appears on the averages, has a very good record, and Callon has also come up with a bound, and Bates and G. Clarke have also greatly improved.

The followiag is the table of averages of those who have played at least four innings, and with an average of three and over :

times Innings not oat runs average. R. Smith 5 ..... .... 0 Bates Clark (A) J J Crostield WE Podmore. 5 7 Callon E England G Clarke W Wordsworth . . 6 81. armor

The second team had even a better result than their seniors, for whereas Oldham 2nd only made the same total as the first, viz : 62, the Warrington second scored 203 for the loss f only seven wickets. This is the first time this season that the first and second teams have been successful on the same day. The second team have a better record than the first, for they have won three, drawn two, and lost one. Brown was in fine form with the bat on Saturday, and he carried his bat out for 56. Rush worth, Duckworth and Adamson each scored over 21 The bowlers figures on the W ar- rington side were remarkable, Duckworth gaining six for 18. Warrington were almost robbed of victory, for they only obtained the last wicket a few minutes before time. Nine Oldham wickets had fallen for only 34 runs, and it was the last wicket which caused the greatest trouble.

Lancashire cut up very badly before Surrey, and they suffered a crushing defeat at the hands of the Southerners. The game was really thrown away during the first half hour of the match when Tinsley and Ward were run out so easily. Ward's loss was especially great to Lancashire for he is in such fine form at present that he might have saved the match, He showed what he could do in the second innings by putting on a large score. Being run out is very unsatisfactory, and it is to be hoped that after having three run out in 004 innings the Lancashire men will be a little more careful in the future.

The defeat of Lancashire makes Surrey's chance of the championship much more favourable, although it is rather too early in the season t) think about that yet. The three coußties which ari most in the runniniY at present are Lancashire, Surrey, and Yorkshire, and it is most probable that the champion county this year will be one of these three.

By the defeat of Hampshire on Saturday by Yorkshire there is now no unbeaten county. The mast unfortu- nate county is Somerset who have not yet won a single match, and yet the greater majority of their beatings have only been very narrow ones, and the match with Middlesex was taken from them by time, when the game would otherwise have been a certainty for them. With a little better fortune they will come with a rush. The Sankey Wesleyans and Sankey Wire Mills each came to the top on Saturday, and each of their victims was a Penketh team—the Penketh Wesleyana and Pen- keth Friends respectively. The Wesleyan% victory was the most pronounced as it was by 11 to 42 There was some good scoring by Sankey Bridges, Sudlow contri- buting 15, Roderick 14, and Taylor 10. It is rather sin- gular that the two top scorers should he run out. Only one Penketh player, reached double figures. Llewellyn carried off the bowling honours,for his seven wickets only cost 16 runs. Should he keep up this form he would be a decided acquisition to the team.

The performance of the Wire Mills was a very meri- torious one, and there are not many teams who can say that they have beaten Penketh Friends, but this is what Wire Mills did on Saturday. The Mills did very batilyto start with, and despite good stands by Williams and Woodcock, and Simpson and Hobson, the total realised was only 58. The Friends only came to within nine of this, and Warburton was to a lar,re extent responqible for this, his four wickets costing the small total of four runs. Simpson and Hobson shared the other wickets.

The Y.M.C.A.7s of Warrington and St. Helens had a pleasant match and pleasanter tea together on Saturday. The match was a decisive victory for Warrington. but each side did full justice to the tea which was provided the kindness of Mr. J. Fulton Smith, the president.

Peter Walker and Sons first and second elevens have had a good time of it recently, and *heir opponents of last Saturday have no occasion te look hack on their matches with feelings of elevation and pride. They first engaged the C.L.C. Engineers and dismisSed them for the paltry sum of 19, six of the engineers not contribut- ing a run, and then they put on a score of 80. The second antagonised Oughtrington Park, and dismissed the Park for the still more paltry sum of 10, seven con- tributing "ducks," and then scored 74. They ought to be a little more merciful.

Lancashire have to some extent atoned for their de- j feat by Surrey by their brilliant victory over Notts on Tuesday. It was rather hard on Flowers, who was taking his benefit at the match that the game should be brought to such an abrupt termination, but still, it waa his misfortune,and he must not blame Mold, but rather the inability of the Notts batsmen to withstand the ex- presses which that player sent down. Mold simply sur- passed himself on Tuesday, and that is saving a good deal. It is a great pity that he was injured and unable to play during the greater part of the Surrey match, and even while he was playing, he could not do full jus- tice to himself, otherwise the result ought to have been much different.

By defeating Yorkshire. Surrey keep the top place in the championship table, but according to the number of matches played, Lancashire have still the be.t record. Essex and Werwickvhire have each won their first victory, and now there are only Kent and Somerset who cannot boast of a single victory.

W. G. Grace has rather spoiled his average by the Warwickshire match,and he will have to score a century or two more if he wants to keep at the top of the table of averages. Albert Ward is slowly creeping up the table, and is now the fourth. He plays in quch corsis- tent form that it is within the range of possibility that he will occupy the top position of the averages before very long.

The Grace testimonial is assaming gigantic propor- tions, and it is probable that it will not simply be a national movement,but a cosmopolitan one. It is enough to make one's mouth water to think of the amount of money which is flowing in for the doctor's benefit. He will without doubt receive the highest amount of money a cricketer has ever received, even by a professional. I am pleased to notice that the Mayor, not to be behind- hand,haa contributed 100 shillings to the swelling fund.

THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY, JUNE 22 1894

SWIM2dIMG.

On Wednesday aftern-)on a swimming belt and the championship of the Warrington Grammar Schoot were competed for in the Corporation Baths, Legh-street. Mr. Barlow acted as starter and judge, and the winner ac- complished the distance in 3inins. and ssecs. /he com- petition serves to stimulate the desire for proficiency in the art of natation and is commendable on that ac- count.

The first annual club handicap of the Warrington Swimming Club was swum off in the baths on Tuesday evening. Fifty yards was the distance and the winners of the different heats were L. Davies, C. Ashton,Thomp- son, Graham, and Moses Walker. The final will be swum off on Tuesday night on the occasion of the gala. PENKETH.

GREAT SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL—The monthly meet- ing o the Great Sankey Parish Council was held at the Board School, Great Sankey, on Monday evening. Mr. W. P. Skelton (chairman) presided. There were also present—Messrs. Henry Bate (vice-chairman), Thomas Flalton, W. H. Gorse, and Joseph Wright (clerk). A communication from the Warrington Rural District Council was read, which stated that in accordance with a motion proposed by Mr. Halton, the Council had relegated their powers with regard to sanitary matters to the Parish Colincil.—The Chairman said the District Council having relegated their powers to that Council, they had now to consider whether it was necessary they should go into the matter or not. It was rather a serious task to undertake. He had heard no complaints about sanitary matters, nor had there been any request to his knowledge that a change of procedure should take place. Mr. Halton said that as the Parish Council's representative on the District Council he had mem- tioned the fact simply and purely on behalf of builders who were anxious to erect property in Sankey who had no sewerage scheme to assist them in their work. It was very wrong for anyone to contam- inate a brook running through Sankey. They had had complaints from a Mr. Pownall, of Great Sankey, and from different people with reference to the pollution of the brook by refuse.—The Chairman said there was on pollution of any brook in Great Sankey, he was satisfied ! of that—Mr. Halton said be held a contrary opinion. He brought this matter forward in the interests of farmers particularly. A serious complaint had been made by one of their parish councillors that his horses and cattle went to tha brook into which drainage ran to drink. While on the subject he wished to say that he thought the surveyor, whoever he was, ought to be very careful before granting a certificate to anyene coming into the district to build. He ought to see that the sani- tary arrangements were all right.—The Chairman thought that was a matter that was altogether out of their jurisdiction as a Parish Council. He did not think that that authority had anything to do with the passing of plans. If the Council took up the scheme the heavi- est ratepayers in the township, such as the railway and canal companies, could not laa rated, and that would mean that the rateable value of the township would be cut down to one-fourth or one-eighth of what it stood in the rate-book. That would mean a heavy charge on the property owners of the parish, and put them to an expenditure of from 83. to 83. in the L.—After some further conversation it was decided to take no steps in the matter at present.—The Chairman said several letters had been received from the Local Government Board, and one referred to the acquisition of land by parish councils—the allotment scheme. He did not know whether they had advanced so far in the matter of local government as to entertain the idea of the compulsory hiring or purchasing esf land for allotments. If they had, and there was any demand, they had a copy of the regulations from the Local Government Board showing what steps ought to be taken in the matter.—Mr. Gorse asked whether there was any application, to which the Chairman replied in the negative. —The subject then dropped, and some routine business having been disposed of the Council rose.

SHIP CANAL

THE DOCKERS' DISPUTE.

The dockers at Salford who refuse to obey the order of the Ship Canal Company to desist from wearing badges conspicuously when seeking employment, and are prac- tically locked out in consequence, held a demonstration on Sunday afterno m, and a meeting was subsequently held in Steyensen-square Dr. Pankhurst„ barrister, presided, and declared that the Ship Canal directors, in refusing to allow the men to mount their buttons, were striking a blow at the trades unionism of the country, which must he resisted. A resolution was proposed con- demning the action of the directors of the company, from the point of view of the dockers, shareholders, and ratepayers of the city who had lent money to the canal. Mr. J. Sexton, of Liverpool, general secretary of the union, said the real reason why the bade., was objected to was that the union was protecting the lives and limbs of its members, and it was now sought to destroy it. The directors had declared war to the knife and they should have it. The d )ckers would not only block the ships, they would block the barges. The unionists at Glasgow offered to help, hut he had kept them back in the hope that the disputa would be settled peaceably. Mr. Marshall Sevens, the manager, gloated over the fsct that they hail not carried nut their threats. They had not done so because they had not tried yet. They h‘d an instance of their power at Birkenhead last Mon- day, when the ships from Manchester which had called to complete their cargoes were blocked. The resolution was carried THE FOUR PER CENT FIRST MORTGAGE DEBENTURES.

ACCIDENT AT THE VIADUCT WORKS.

On Monday morning whilst a spring maker named John Barrow, employed at the Viaduct Works, was assisting several others to change a heavy iron table, it fell upon one of his legs and fractured it just above the ankle. He was removed home on a stretcher and attended by Dr. Handyside, who set the broken limb. VIADUCT WORKS' OFFICE STAFF PICNIC.

The first annual pic-nic of the office staff of the I Viaduct Works, Earlestown, took place on Satur- day afternoon under most favourable conditions, the place selected for the inauguration being the ancient city of Chester. The party, including several of the works' foremen, numbered 28, and , left Earlestown at 1130, a saloon being provided for their accommodation Chester was reached about 12.40, and a move was at once made for the Coach, and Horses Inn, where a substantial.and excellently served dinner was partaken of and heartily en- joyed. About two o'clock the party departed for the riverside, and embarked on board Mr. Capner's steam launch " Ontario," en route for Eaton Hall. IThe sale up the river was most enjoy able, and on I arrival at Eccleston Ferry the party disembarked, and walked through the grounds of the Duke of Westminster to Eaton Hall, which they duly in- spected, the objects of interest being lucidly ex- plained by one of the guides provided for the pur- pose. A retrograde movement was afterwards made for the ferry, and at 5.45 the return journey to Chester was undertaken, happily without any of the voyagers suffering from the effects of " Mal de mer." Tea was partaken of at the Coach and rforses, and the journey for home was made by the 8.35 train, Earlestown being reached about 930, everybody having thoroughly enjoyed themselves.

GOOD TEMPLAR&

The weekly meeting of the Hope of Earlestown lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall, Earlestown, on Monday evening. Prior to the opening of the adult lodge, the juvenile templars gave an entertainment when there was a good muster of juveniles and adults. Bro. E. Middleton, CS., J.T., of Leigh, presided, and gave an interesting address. A good programme of songs, recitations, and dialogues was given by the children in a very satisfactory manner. An address was also given by Bro. W. France, of Leigh, and the thanks of the meeting were tendered him and Bro. Middleton, after which the juveniles retired, being presented with fruit. The adult lodge was then formally opened, Bro. W. Wilson, C.T., presiding. There was a good attendance of members, and several visitors were present. One candidate was restored to membership. Arrangements were made for paying a visit to Tranmere lodge and it was resolved to f3rm a choir. Other items of business. were transacted, and addresses were given by Bros Middleton and France. NEWTON, HAYDOOK, AND GOLBORNE

BUILDING SOCIETY.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The 34th annual meeting of the shareholders ef the above society was held in the Town Hall, Newton, on Wednesday evening. Mr. J. Sankey presided, and there was a moderate attendance of members.

The Chairman said they had not Mr. Arnott with them that night, as they had han for several years, and he sent a letter of apology.

THE REPORT.

The Secretary then read the annual report as follows : —"The directors have pleasure in reporting to the mem- bers the Society's transactions for the past year,and pre- senting to them the annexed statement of accounts, certified by the auditors. Advances have been made this year on eight lots of property amounting to £4,200, and after deducting repayments the total sum outstand- ing on properties not in hand is £16.195 134. 31. on forty-two lots, and the interest received from them is £742 4s. Bd. The profits for the year are £Bl4 10s. 10d., and a dividend of five pounds per cent, has been de- dared, which will absorb £799 10s. 5d., leaving £3O Os. sd. to be carried to the contingent account, which now stands at £2,340 Is. 7d. This is 91 per cent, on the amount outstanding on properties in the Society's hands, and 12i per cent, on the total sum outstanding on mortgage. The directors are prepared to make ad- vances on liberal terms on eligible freehold or leasehold properties, and in addition to the issuing of invested shares, they are prepared to receive deposits or loans at a fixed rate of interest of 3i per cent., payable half- yearly,and subject to various terms of notice for repay- ment. The directors congratulate the members on the continued prosperity of the Society, and confidently re- commend their attention to the figures in the balance sheet,which show it to be a thoroughly reliable and pro- fitable investment, a dividend of 5 per cent, having been earned during the past year, and there now being a reserve fund of £2,340 Is. 7d. The Society consists of 300 members, 1191dieg 1,647 i invested shares,and 2,27 bi morteage shares." The Chairman moved the adoption of the balance sheet and report. He did not think that he could say anything more to them than what they could see in the report. It recommended itself to them without any re- marks from him, and he expressed the pleasure it gave the managers to be able to put such an excellent report before their members. They had as they would see made advances on 8 lots of property,which he considered was a good piece of work for the year. The other work- ing had been also satisfactory, and to prove that it was satisfactory they had declared a dividend of 5 per cent, which he thought was a good one; it was not easily earned at these times,but they had earned it, and earned it well, and they put a further sum of over £3O to the contingent account,which left them with a reserve fund, that was almost equal to the amount of money that was on the property already in their hands. Everything seemed to him to be favourable to the Society. They were getting fresh investors which was very satisfactory. They must, he supposed, keep on plodding as they had been doing. They were always ready to entertain any proposals that might come before them, and he thought most of them knew that they had facilities that very few societies if any, had. They had new rules, made up to date, and they worked the society on as little ex- pense as possible. If they went on, as they had done in the past year,no investor would have any need to regret belonging to the Newton Building Society—(Applause)— Mr. Lawton, seconded.—Mr. Singleton said the classof borrowers who now came before the managers seemed to be those people that a building society, was really created to meet. Another point which was favourable, was, that the directors were constantly taking up fresh shares, and when they were not doing that, they seemed to be bringing their own relatives and personal friends to take up shares.—Mr. Appleby (auditor) also spoke as to the sound financial condition of the Society.—Mr. H. J. Langton (solicitor) was pleased to be present that night. He thought five per cent, was a high rate of interest. The Building Society's Act which had just been passed would cause Mr. Appleby slightly more work than in the past, but as that society had been incorporated under the Societies' Act of 1874, it would make very little difference to them. The Act would benefit the Society, because it would crush out retten concerns which were badly managed and insolvent. It would prevent mushroom Societies from investing their money improperly, and in his opinion the people would have greater confidence in Building Societies.—The motion was thou put and un- animously adopted. Messrs. J. Crouchlev, Joseph Jackson and Thomas Wilkinson were re-elected airectors. Messrs. Appleby and I. Bateman were re-appointed auditors, and Mr. A. Blything, treasurer. On the motion of Mr. Appleby, seconded by Mr. Longton, a vote of thanks was accorded the trustees for their services.

The following vote was also passed "That this meeting expresses its deep sympathy with Mr. McCorquodale in his serious illness, and hopes he may have a speedy re- covery, to s.rve the Society for many years as trustee." A vote of thanks to the Chairman terminated the meeting.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

THURSDAY.

In the House of Commons on Thursday evening Mr. W. Joaxson asked whether the Government would grant the site f-r the proposed CROMWELL statue to a Commit- tee who desired to raise a memorial of the Protector by private subscription' and Sir W. HARCOURT said the Government would take the matter into consideration. Mr. A. MORLEY sated in reply to Mr. SCHWANN that he had given instructions for stopping foreign lottery circulars in covers received at the Post-office, and for returning them to the country where they were posted. The House again went into Committee on the Welsh Church Bill, resuming consideration of clause 6, which deals with the disposition of property by the Commis- sioners. Mr Loma moved an amendment to vest tithe rent charge in the parish council or the county borough council. Mr. ASQUITH, in opposing the amend- ment, said he was anxious to introduce such safeguards as were necessary to compel county councils to have re- gard to the interests of the parishes concerned, and when they came to the ninth clause he believed he should be able to satisfy the hon. member. The amend- ment was withdrawn. An amendment moved by Vis- count CRANBORNE, that all burial grounds should be vested in the Representative Body of the Church as soon as they were legally closed, was rejected by 206 to 184 ; and another amendment, moved by Mr. GRIF- Frru•Boscewm that burial grounds not separated by a carriage road should go with the church, was defeated by 135 to 113. Mr. GRIFFMI•BOSCAWEN moved an amendment to provide that a p irish eouncil should ad- minister only such glebe as was situated in its own ' parish. It was defeated by 151 to 131. Mr. D. THOMAS moved an amendment to omit the sub section providing that the Commissioners should vest the tithe rent-charge in the county council of the county in which the land was situated. After a rather heated discussion in re- gard to suppositious exceptional cases Mr. ASQUITH said he intended to propose a sub-section dealing with this matter. _

On a division the amendment was rejected by 172 to 165.

Opposition was taken to Sir J. HIBBEELT'S motion after midnight in reference to the Committee for the Friendly Societies Bill, and it had to be postponed.

RUNCORN ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL CLUB.

ANNUAL MEETING.

Mr. H. G. Grounds presided at the annual general meeting of the members of the above club on Monday evening. There was a good atteudance.—The Secretary submitted his report for the past season which showed that the first team had played 13 matches away, five being won and nine lost, whilst four away fixtures had been cancelled. 24 goals were scored for,and 59 against. In the home engagements the team had fared rather better, haying played 10, won six, and lost four, whilst 11 home fixtures had been cancelled ; 38 goals being scored for and 17 against. The goals were scored as follows :—W. Peacock (captain) 26, J. K. Jones 6, C. Burrows 6, W. Baylie 5, W. Walker 5, St Walker 3, H. Pye 3, W. Hough 2, and C. Limb, J. Hampson,F. Linton, W. Blower, J. Linton and W. Carter one each. The second team who like the first had suffered greatly through home fixtures being cancelled during the severe weather, had played 13 matches, won eight, lost three. and scored 35 goals, whilst 23 had been scored against them. The following were responsible for the scoring : —C. Burrows 6, J. Parry 6, F. Linton 5, F Upton 4, 0. Garvie 4, J. Linton 2, J. Bell 2, and J. Clarke, F. Bell, J. Jones, W. Baylie and W. Peacock one each.—The Trea- surer stated that the income during the season bad amounted to £2l 15s. Bd.' whilst the expenses had been £25 7s. 04d., thus leaving a balance due to treasurer of £3 lie. aid. He attributed the loss to severe weather which had greatly affected the home matches. The election of officers for the season 1895-6 was then proceeded with. Mr. S. B. Chadwick, J.P., was unanimously re-elected president, and Messrs. W. E. Hough, H. MaLeod and W. Peacock, were re-elected to the respective offices of secretary and treasurer, and captain of Ist team without opposition. Voting by ballot having been taken, the Chairman announced that the following persons had been elected to fill the under- mentioned offices :—Sub-captain,lst team, R. Ledsham ; committee, Messrs. J. Hampson, G. H. Grounds, W. Baylie, J. Leath wood and J. K 4 Jones ; umpires' Messrs. ' J. Lea.thwood (Ist team). T. Buxton (2nd team) ; audi- tors, Messrs. Burrows and E. Gleave. The question of joining the Warrington and District League was next discussed, and ultimately after full consideration of the matter, it was ultimately resolved that application be made for admission to the League. A resolution was unanimously passed to the following effect :—"That the heartiest congratulations and best wishes of the members of the Runcorn Association Football Club be forwarded to Sir T. Brunner, Bart, M.P., on the distinguished honour which has recently been conferred on him by her Majesty the Queen." A vote of thanks to the Chairman and to the several officers for their services during the season, terminated the proceedings.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of the Newton Urban District Council was held in the Earlestown Town Hall, on Wedaesday, when the chair was occupied by Mr. C. B. F. Borrun, J.P., C.C., and there were also present the following members of the Council :—Messrs.T.Ormand, W. Collingwood,P. MacMahon,J. Simpson,W.Thompson, jun.. J. Smith, J.P., J. Slee, E. T. Evans, and the Rev. E. W.W. Kaye; also Messrs. J. E. Worsley (law clerk), R. Rrierley (clerk and surveyor), W.Speakman, W.Lane, and H. Houghton. The Chairman said that that was the first annual meeting of the District Council, and the Law Clerk advised them that it was only a formal meeting. They met to place the accounts before the public,and formerly it had been the custom to elect auditors to go over them. Their Law Clerk, however, told them that in future the Local Government Board would appoint auditor;. It would be as well to appoint auditors for the present year until the Local Government Board appointed them. The accounts submitted were for three months of the present Council, the rest being for the old Board of Commissioners. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR).

The vexed question of setts versus macadam which has agitated the old Board of Coro missionrrs for several years was brought to a conclusion at the last meeting of the District Council. As to whether the decision is a wise one, there is sure of course to be a divergence of opinion, but as to the merits of the two road materials, there cannot possibly be any manner of doubt. It has been proved over and ,iver again that for streets over which there is much traffic, setts are far and away before macadam. The first cost of the latter is much below that of setts, but, the cost of maintenance is greatly in excess, and in the end it will be found that street making by setts is by far the cheapest process.

The changeable weather hereabouts seems to be affecting the weak-chested people who live in this district. This is more noticeable on Sunday even- ings within the various places of worship. It is painful indeed, when the time comes for the minis- ter to ascend the pulpit, to observe what a large number of people suddenly find that they are affilcted witn irritating coughs. Ft only needs a start, and it would appear to an observant listener that almost everybody in the church seemed anxious to join in the contest, and, strange though it may seem, the contest rages most fiercely just at I the point when the preacher is making a telling peroration. Now I think an excellent plan wouki be for the wardens and deacons of the different sanctums, to appoint some reasonable person to promenade during the sermon, with a box, contain- ing some renowned cough lozenges, which he could hand to those who join in the endeavour to inter- rupt the preacher.

The Liberal meeting which was held in the Town Hall last week, was of a most enthusiastic and en- couraging character, and augurs well for the pros- pects of the Liberal candidate. Mr. Mon was well received and his address given in a lucid and tell- ing manner, was listened to with the greatest at- tention. It was admitted that the coming tight would be an uphill one,but this may be rendered easier with the organisation in a perfect condition. The new registration agent has now taken up his residence in the division, and when he gets well into his duties.he will doubtless be able to put the voters lists on a proper footing.

A NOVEL INDUSTRY AT COOLGATtDIE

According to information received by the " Frank. furter Zeitung,” a fortune is being made in the Wect Australian goldfields by Mr. Bennett, not by gold dig- ging, but by a bicycle post. Coolgardie, the centre of 1 the mining world, lies 280 miles distant from Dunda and every Friday Mr. Bennett starts from Coolgardie oti his bicycle, and proceeds to his first station, a small settlement fifty miles away. Next morning he mounts his wheel again and travels all day,halting•for the eight aad thus proceeds until Dundas is reached, with aston- ishing punctuality, in spite of the badness of the roads. His wheel is provided with a letter-box, so arranged that a quantity of newspapers can be packed upon its lid. Below the bicycle hangs a water bottle. Packets are also fastened on each side of the handle. A knapsack on the postman's back contains further material, aml hanging to his belt is a leather bag intended for tele- grams, and containing besides a sharp knife and re- volver. For each letter he carries he receives a ehilh ng, for each telegram five shillings, and for a sub,criptii in of two shillings a week he carries the subscribers whole correspondence.
